I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
The Applicant, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, submitted a new drug application (NDA) for the novel
oral fixed-dose combination of vanzacaftor (VNZ), tezacaftor (TEZ), and deutivacaftor (D-IVA)
for the treatment of cystic fibrosis (CF) in patients aged 6 years and older who have at least one
F508del (F) variant1 or another response variant in the cystic fibrosis transmembrane
conductance regulator (CFTR) gene. The NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary team. Each
team recommends approval for patients with CF aged 6 years and older with at least one F508del
variant or another responsive variant in the CFTR gene, and the signatory authority for this
application concurs with these recommendations.
To support the NDA for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, the Applicant submitted the results from two phase
3, randomized, double-blind, noninferiority trials: VX20-121-102 (Trial 102) and VX20-121-103
(Trial 103). Both trials demonstrated the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to
elexacaftor/tezacaftor/ivacaftor (ELX/TEZ/IVA), the current standard of care in this population,
as measured by pulmonary function in subjects aged 12 years and older with CF. The primary
endpoint was percent predicted forced expiratory volume in 1 second (ppFEV1) through 24
weeks, an established endpoint for this indication. Additional secondary endpoints supported
these results. The efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is extrapolated to patients aged 6 through 11
years of age from the adolescent and adult populations of Trials 102 and 103. Additional support
comes from exploratory efficacy endpoint data from the open-label trial, Trial VX21-121-105
(Trial 105), in the 6- to 11-year-old population.
The available safety database was adequate for review and demonstrated that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
is safe for its intended use as labeled. The data support that the risks of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA are
comparable to those of ELX/TEZ/IVA and include the potential risk for drug-induced liver
injury and liver failure. The risk of hepatoxicity will be managed by the inclusion of a Boxed
Warning in labeling and increased monitoring through an enhanced pharmacovigilance strategy
that will attach to this approval. The remaining observed adverse reactions were like those for
ELX/TEZ/IVA and will be addressed through labeling and routine pharmacovigilance.
The NDA also included appropriate preapproval nonclinical and clinical pharmacology studies.
A nonclinical postmarketing requirement and clinical pharmacology drug-drug interaction
postmarketing requirement will attach to this approval.

1 Throughout this review the term “variant” will be used per the most recent American College of Genetics and

Genomics (ACMG) standards (Richards et al. 2015; den Dunnen et al. 2016). Note that in the United States
Prescribing Information (USPI) for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and Applicant materials, the term “mutation” is used. In the
context of this review, these terms are synonymous.

4
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
The review team concludes that the benefit of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, based on noninferiority to
ELX/TEZ/IVA as measured by pulmonary function, outweighs the risks when VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
is used as recommended in the approved labeling. There are no outstanding issues from any
review discipline. The signatory concurs with the content of the various discipline assessments
and their recommendations for approval. The Agency and Applicant have agreed upon the final
labeling language. The submitted clinical program is adequate to support the efficacy and safety
of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA for the treatment of CF in patients 6 years of age and older with a copy of
the F508del variant or another responsive CFTR variant. VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA will offer an
alternative, once-daily therapy for many patients with CF and will be a new therapy for some
patients with certain CFTR variants. The action for this application will be Approval.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigations.
To support the proposed indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, the Applicant completed two phase 3
efficacy and safety trials, Trials 102 and 103. The trials demonstrated SEE for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
in subjects with CF with a responsive CFTR variant. The determination of efficacy is based on
demonstration of the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA on the primary
endpoint of the absolute change from baseline in the ppFEV1 through 24 weeks. Evaluation of in
vitro data from Ussing chamber electrophysiology experiments demonstrated greater activity for
the triple drug combination, VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, compared to the mono-components and dualcomponents.
The proposed indication includes 303 variants in the CFTR gene, representing over 90% of the
U.S. CF population. To support the inclusion of the individual CFTR variants, the Applicant
submitted a combination of clinical data, in vitro data, and a proposal for the extrapolation of
safety and efficacy to rare noncanonical splice (NCS) variants. The data types and the
conclusions on their contributions to SEE are reviewed below and summarized in Table 2.

Abbreviations: CFTR, cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elexacaftor; FRT, Fischer rat
thyroid; HBE, human bronchial epithelial; IVA, ivacaftor; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor

Trials 102 and 103 were adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trials that enrolled 971 subjects
aged 12 years and older with CF. Among the enrolled population, 95.7% of subject had a CFTR
genotype that included at least one copy of the F508del variant, the most common variant in the
U.S. CF population. Both Trials 102 and 103 demonstrated the noninferiority of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA with respect to the ppFEV1 through 24 weeks. The
noninferiority trial design and margin selected were reasonable.
In addition, Trials 102 and 103 enrolled 42 subjects with 23 non-F508del triple combination
responsive (i.e., responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA based on in vitro data) variants. The subgroup
analysis of these subjects was limited by sample size but was consistent with the overall
population and supportive of the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA in this
population. Approval for this group of variants is also supported by in vitro data from prior
development programs.
To support an additional 259 CFTR variants, the Applicant submitted in vitro data utilizing the
Fischer Rat Thyroid (FRT) cell system, a type of Ussing Chamber electrophysiology assay the
Division has experience leveraging for regulatory actions for CF therapies. These data supported
the inclusion of 154 CFTR variants that were previously demonstrated to be responsive to
ELX/TEZ/IVA, TEZ/IVA, and/or IVA. These data also supported 31 new CFTR variants not
responsive to currently approved therapies, as well as 74 CFTR variants that were recently added
to the ELX/TEZ/IVA indication as part of a supplemental NDA uploaded to DARRTS on
December 11, 2024 (DARRTS ID: 5493844).
Additional in vitro data utilizing a human bronchial epithelial (HBE) cell system, another Ussing
Chamber assay, supported the inclusion of a single variant, N1303K. HBE cells were used
instead of FRT because the N1303K variant is not responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA in the FRT
system. A regulatory threshold to define effectiveness for this assay was established as part of
the recently approved efficacy supplement for ELX/TEZ/IVA. The determination of
effectiveness was made by comparing the response to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA with the response to
TEZ/IVA in F508del/F508del HBE cells.
The efficacy and safety for 19 NCS variants were extrapolated from the overall population
evaluated in Trials 102 and 103. These variants are expected to result in the production of small
quantities of wild-type (normal function) CFTR, and extrapolation was supported, in part, by in
vitro FRT data demonstrating that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA augments wild-type CFTR function. In
addition, there is clinical data demonstrating the benefit of ELX/TEZ/IVA in certain variants
included in this group (Trial VX21-445-124, supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 and
217660/S003, uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]). Together,

these data support the conclusion that the expected response to treatment and benefits of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA would translate to patients with these rare variants.
In addition, the efficacy and safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA were extrapolated from the adolescent
and adult population to CF patients aged 6 through 11 years of age with a responsive CFTR
variant. Extrapolation is supported by comparable drug systemic exposures between the 6- to 11year-old population and the adolescent and adult population and the knowledge that CF is the
same disease across age groups.

CF is a rare, progressive, and life-shortening genetic disease.
CF is a rare, progressive, life-shortening autosomal
The pathologic changes associated with CF start early in life.

The CFTR variants included in the proposed indication

pathologic variants in the CFTR gene that lead to

regulation of salt and water absorption and secretion
throughout the body. Lack of properly functioning CFTR
leads to the clinical sequelae of CF disease: malabsorption
of nutrients, inability to mobilize tenacious respiratory
secretions, recurrent pulmonary infections, irreversible lung

majority of these pathologic changes start at birth. The
median age of survival for a patient with CF is in the mid40s.

States, approximately 85% of CF patients carry at least one
allele and 45% are homozygous for this variant.

The clinical benefit of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was demonstrated to be
noninferior to ELX/TEZ/IVA for CF patients aged 12 years and

and the adolescent and adult populations and the knowledge

noncanonical splice variants not feasible to enroll in clinical
trials because of rarity and not amenable to established in vitro

Conclusions and Reasons

Risk and risk
management

•

The safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA appears similar to that of
ELX/TEZ/IVA and includes the potential risk for DILI and liver
failure.

•
•

•

•

•

The VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA AEs observed were generally
consistent with the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
Specific safety analyses were performed, including
analyses for liver function test elevation, rash, creatinine
phosphokinase elevation, and neuropsychiatric events.
The safety data submitted suggest that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
conveys a similar risk to ELX/TEZ/IVA, although the overall
analysis is limited by the lack of placebo arm to assess the
baseline rates of events.
A new signal for life-threatening drug-induced liver injury
was recently characterized for ELX/TEZ/IVA, and the
available data for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA suggest the risk is likely
similar. Limitations of the available data include lack of
placebo arm and the ELX/TEZ/IVA run-in period for both
Trials 102 and 103, which resulted in only ELX/TEZ/IVAtolerant subjects eligible for enrollment.
The potential for DILI related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was
carefully evaluated in consultation with the Division of
Hepatology and Nutrition. Rates of maximum transaminase
elevations were greater for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared to
ELX/TEZ/IVA. We also identified five cases of
nonjaundiced DILI events that were adjudicated as
probably or possibly related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
The safety results from Trial 105 did not reveal any new
safety signals or identify imbalances in AEs to suggest
differential risks for children compared to adolescents or
adults. However, given the single arm design and small
sample size, interpretation of these results is limited.

A Boxed Warning for drug-induced liver injury and liver failure
will be included in labeling, and increased postmarket
surveillance will occur through an enhanced pharmacovigilance
strategy. A REMS is not indicated at this time. There were no
safety signals identified in the development program for
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA that outweigh the potential benefit for the
treatment of CF.
Safety for the 6- through 11-year-old CF population was
extrapolated from the adolescent and adult populations enrolled
in the phase 3 development program based upon comparable
systemic exposures.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
CF is a rare, progressive genetic condition that leads to premature respiratory failure caused by
pathologic variants in the CFTR gene. The majority of patients in the United States are currently
eligible for one of the four approved medications that affect CFTR processing or function Table
4. There remains an unmet need for new therapies for patients with CF, including those with
ineligible CFTR variants and those who are intolerant of or incompletely treated by currently
available therapies. VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is a fixed-dose combination product with the same
mechanism of action as ELX/TEZ/IVA. D-IVA increases the channel open probability of the
CFTR channel, and VNZ and TEZ improve intracellular protein processing to allow for
increased protein expression at the cell surface, thus resulting in an increase in overall apical
anion transport.
To support the NDA for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, the Applicant submitted the results from two phase
3, randomized, double-blind, ELX/TEZ/IVA-controlled, noninferiority trials. Both trials, VX20121-102 (Trial 102) and VX20-121-103 (Trial 103) evaluated subjects with CF aged 12 years
and older over 52 weeks and demonstrated the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to
ELX/TEZ/IVA with respect to pulmonary function as measured by the absolute change in
ppFEV1 through 24 weeks. Additional secondary endpoints, including the rate of CF pulmonary
exacerbations and the change in the cystic fibrosis questionnaire-revised respiratory domain,
support the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Interpretation of these endpoints is limited by lack of
multiplicity control, but the results are generally supportive of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and are
discussed in more detail in Section 6.
In addition, the Applicant submitted the results of secondary endpoints related to sweat chloride
(SwCl) concentrations. SwCl is an important pharmacodynamic marker of CFTR function;
however, endpoints based on SwCl are not clinically meaningful as they do not measure how a
patient feels, functions, or survives and have not been validated as surrogate endpoints. As such,
they cannot be used to demonstrate comparative efficacy. Both phase 3 trials demonstrated a
statistically significant reduction in the change from baseline in SwCl with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
treatment compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA; however, it is unclear how these changes translate to
clinically meaningful outcomes. The Applicant also submitted results of responder analyses
assessing the proportion of subjects with a week 24 SwCl below 60mmol/L and below
30mmol/L, which were also statistically significant in the prespecified analyses. The data
submitted to support the clinical relevance of these responder analyses, however, was
insufficient. Overall, the SwCl endpoints support that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA has comparable on
target pharmacodynamic effects to ELX/TEZ/IVA. For additional discussion of SwCl, refer to
Section 6.3.2.
There was an adequate safety database to support this review. However, a significant limitation
of the safety database stemmed from the noninferiority design, which eliminated a placebo arm
and necessitated enrolled subjects be tolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA, including during a four-week
run-in period. Since 86.3% of the population had been prescribed ELX/TEZ/IVA or a medication
containing its components prior to enrollment, the generalizability of safety results to
ELX/TEZ/IVA naïve or intolerant patients is limited. Overall, the available data support that the
safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is similar to that of ELX/TEZ/IVA. The potential risk of liver injury
11
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
related to VNZ/TEX/D-IVA was a major focus of the safety review. Overall, there were higher
rates of transaminase elevation and liver-related adverse events leading to drug discontinuation
in subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared to ELX/TEZ IVA. There were no cases of
Hy’s Law or liver failure reported, but there were five cases of nonjaundiced drug-induced liver
injury adjudicated as probably or possibly related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA exposure. A
multidisciplinary team recommended that the potential risk for drug-induced liver injury and
liver failure with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA be communicated to prescribers in a Boxed Warning with
further monitoring through an enhanced pharmacovigilance strategy. Refer to Section 7.7.2 for
details.
The efficacy and safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA were extrapolated to the 6- to 11-year-old
population from the adolescent and adult population studied in Trials 102 and 103. The
Applicant submitted an interim report of the final clinical data from the 6- to 11-year-old cohort
from Trial VX21-121-105 (Trial 105), an open-label, multicohort, 2-part trial in pediatric CF
subjects aged 1 through 11 years. Extrapolation of efficacy and safety in this population is
justified because systemic exposures between the 6- to 11-year-old population and the adolescent
and adult populations are comparable and because CF is the same disease across age groups.
Exploratory efficacy endpoints from Trial 105 were also supportive of the efficacy of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in this population, and a review of safety data from Trial 105 did not reveal
any new concerning safety signals in this population.
The data submitted to support the satisfaction of 21 Code of Federal Regulations 300.50, the
‘combination rule’ was considered adequate and included the clinical demonstration of
noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA, as well as the available historical clinical
trial data with IVA, TEZ/IVA, and ELX/TEZ/IVA. Further support comes from in vitro data
demonstrating a greater effect of the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA triple combination versus the monocomponents and dual-components alone. Refer to Section 6.3.1 for further discussion.
To support the CFTR variant based indication, the Applicant submitted adequate data for the
individual variants. This included in vitro electrophysiology models utilizing FRT and CF HBE
cells, as well as adequate support to justify the potential for benefit in 19 rare NCS variants that
are not amenable to enrollment in clinical trials because of relative rarity and cannot be evaluated
in existing in vitro models. Refer to Section 6.3.3 for further discussion.
The overall risk-benefit assessment supports the approval of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA for the treatment
of CF in patients aged 6 years and old with a copy of F508del or other responsive variants in the
CFTR gene.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Cystic fibrosis (CF) is an autosomal recessive, progressive disease affecting over 33,000 people
in the United States and 90,000 people worldwide (CFF 2023). CF is caused by pathologic
variants in the cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator (CFTR) gene that leads to
decreased or dysfunctional CFTR protein, an anion channel in the apical epithelial membrane. In
the lungs, the CF-mediated alterations in anion and fluid transport result in the accumulation of
thick, tenacious mucus leading to a chronic cycle of inflammation, obstruction, and infection that
ultimately progresses to premature respiratory failure, the leading cause of death in this
population (CFF 2023). In addition, there are significant extrapulmonary manifestations
characteristic of CF, including malabsorption, chronic sinus disease, poor bone health, liver
dysfunction, and a form of diabetes (CFF 2023).
There are over 700 variants in the CFTR gene that are associated with CF disease causation,
although over 2,000 variants have been identified (CFF; JHU; and HSC 2024). The most
common CF-causing variant in the United States is F508del (F), a variant that results in improper
protein folding leading to increased degradation (Cheng et al. 1990). In CF patients with
genotype information available in the United States, approximately 85% of individuals are
heterozygous for F508del and 44% of individuals are F508del homozygous (CFF 2023). The
reported prevalence of the next most commonly reported CFTR variants in the United States is
significantly lower with no other variant reported in >5.0% of the population in 2023 (CFF
2023).
Currently, there is no cure for CF and, historically, CF-directed therapies were focused on the
relief of symptoms and sequelae of the disease. More recently, medications targeting the
underlying pathophysiologic defect in CFTR function and general intracellular protein
processing of CFTR have been developed and approved for CF subpopulations based on CFTR
variants as reviewed below in Table 4.

Ivacaftor (IVA, Trade name: KALYDECO), the first medication targeting CFTR function, was
approved in 2012 but limited at that time to patients aged 6 years and older with a G551D CFTR
variant, the third most prevalent CFTR variant reported in the United States
(Vertex Pharmaceuticals 2012). Initial expansion of the IVA indication to additional variants was
based on clinical data; however, given the rarity of remaining variants, subsequent indication
expansions were based on in vitro studies leveraging Ussing chamber electrophysiology
experiments with Fischer Rat Thyroid (FRT) cells expressing normal and mutant CFTR protein
(Durmowicz et al. 2018). Subsequently, all other medications that directly affect CFTR
processing or function have had the indications expanded to include additional variants using
FRT data that demonstrate an increase in chloride transport by ≥10% of normal from baseline. At
the time of this review, approximately 92% of U.S. CF patients are eligible for a medication that
affects CFTR processing or function based on their genotype and age (CFF 2023).
Elexacaftor/tezacaftor/ivacaftor (ELX/TEZ/IVA), approved in 2019 (Vertex Pharmaceuticals
2019), accounted for 93% of prescriptions for medications that affect CFTR processing or
function reported in 2023 and is standard-of-care therapy for those with eligible CFTR variants
(CFF 2023). It is the most effective therapy for CF that targets the underlying defect in the
disease.
These medications have provided significant clinical benefits to many patients with CF with
responsive CFTR variants (Volkova et al. 2020). However, despite these advances, there remains
a significant ongoing disease burden in CF and the CF population would benefit from safe and
effective alternative therapies.
Vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor (VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA), an oral fixed-dose combination (FDC)
tablet, has the same mechanism of action as ELX/TEZ/IVA. As part of its clinical development
program, ELX/TEZ/IVA had demonstrated a large, sustained clinical effect on the forced
14
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV1), a clinically meaningful endpoint for which there is
regulatory precedent in CF (Heijerman et al. 2019; Middleton et al. 2019). Given the severity of
the underlying disease, the effect size of ELX/TEZ/IVA, and its market prevalence within the CF
population, the Applicant and Division agreed upon a clinical development program that
included two pivotal trials designed to assess the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared
to ELX/TEZ/IVA. The trial design and assessment of the noninferiority margin are reviewed in
Section 6.2.2.1 with an in-depth review of the clinical protocols included in Section 15.1.

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The Applicant submitted data from two phase 3 clinical trials, Trials 102 and 103, as the primary
support for the safety and efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in CF. These trials were both
international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, ELX/TEZ/IVA-controlled trials evaluating
the efficacy and safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA over 52 weeks. The design of the phase 3 clinical
development program is reasonable. The in-depth review of the clinical protocols is included in
Section 15.1.
In addition, the Applicant submitted an interim analysis from Trial 105 to support the use of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in pediatric CF patients ages 6 through 11. Trial 105 is an ongoing, two-part,
open-label, multicohort, pediatric, pharmacokinetic (PK) and safety study of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
in CF subjects 1 through 11 years of age. The submitted interim analysis included the final
clinical data for the 6- to 11-year-old cohorts (Cohorts A1 and B1). An in-depth review of the

clinical protocol for Trial 105 is included in Section 15.2. As with other similar medications in
this population, the efficacy and safety in the 6- to 11-year-old CF population is primarily
extrapolated from adolescents and adults. The efficacy-related endpoints and safety profile from
the 6- to 11-year-old cohorts were also reviewed in comparison to the adolescent and adult data
to assess for consistency or the presence of new signals.
FDA biostatisticians performed efficacy analyses to confirm the data shown in support of the
Applicant’s primary and secondary efficacy endpoints. The review of the efficacy of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is in Section 6. The clinical reviewer and FDA clinical data scientist

reviewers from the Division of Applied Regulatory Science, reviewed dose ranging trials and in
vitro analyses used to support efficacy as well as data supporting the contribution of each
component to the fixed-dose combination, found in Section 6.3.1.
The approach to establishing substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) is reviewed in
Section 3.3, and a summary of the clinical trials submitted by the Applicant in support of the
efficacy and safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is reviewed below in Table 5.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
VNZ and TEZ bind to different sites on the CFTR protein and have an additive effect in
facilitating the cellular processing and trafficking of select mutant forms of CFTR (including
F508del-CFTR) to increase the amount of CFTR protein delivered to the cell surface compared
to either molecule alone. Similar to IVA, deuterated ivacaftor (D-IVA) potentiates the channel
open probability (or gating) of the CFTR protein at the cell surface. The combined effect of
VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA is increased quantity and function of CFTR at the cell surface, resulting
22
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
in increased CFTR activity as measured by CFTR-mediated chloride transport in CF human
bronchial epithelial (HBE) cell cultures derived from donor CF lungs.
In vivo pharmacodynamic studies were not performed. The totality of in vitro pharmacology data
submitted was considered sufficient to characterize the pharmacological activity of VNZ, TEZ,
and IVA and inform labeling for the indication of CF.

The active moieties are VNZ, TEZ, M1-TEZ (similar potency compared to TEZ), D-IVA, and M1-D-IVA (20% of potency
compared to D-IVA).
QT prolongation
Following a single dose of 60 mg VNZ (Form A, which resulted in six times the Cmax value when compared to the Cmax
following 20 mg VNZ dose in Form D in FDC tablet), no clinically relevant effects on prolongation of the QTc interval were
observed in the thorough QT study, Study VX22-121-013.
Refer to the approved label of ELX/TEZ/IVA (Trade name: TRIKAFTA). At doses up to three times the maximum
recommended dose of TEZ and IVA, the QT/QTc interval in healthy subjects was not prolonged to any clinically relevant
extent. In vitro results showed that IVA inhibits about 22% of hERG current at 8 µM. Given the hydrogen to deuterium
replacements from IVA to D-IVA, which is unlikely to change its 3-dimentional conformation, the high plasma protein binding
of IVA and D-IVA (>99%), and the in vivo Cmax,ss value around 3 – 6 µM for IVA and D-IVA, it is reasonable to extrapolate the
in vivo QT results from IVA to D-IVA following the therapeutic dose of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.

ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Characteristic
Dose
proportionality

Drug Information
Study VX17-121-001 Part A showed that using suspension formulation, VNZ exposure increases dose proportionally from 5
to 60 mg following single dose administration (i.e., AUCinf increases 14.6-fold when dose increases 12-fold) in healthy
subjects. Part B from the same study showed VNZ exposure increases slightly greater than dose proportionally following
administration of multiple oral doses of VNZ QD for 10 days, which VNZ AUCtau increased 8.5-fold from 10 mg QD to 60 mg
QD.
Refer to the clinical pharmacology review of TEZ/IVA (dated Feb 2, 2018), exposures of tezacaftor (administered alone or in
combination with ivacaftor) increase in an approximately dose proportional manner with increasing doses from 10 mg to
300 mg once daily.

Accumulation

Study VX18-561-001 showed that D-IVA exposure increases dose proportionally with ascending dose from 50 to 250 mg
following 5 days once daily treatment (AUCtau increases 6-fold).
Study VX17-121-001 showed accumulation ratio of AUCtau ranged 3.5-5.5 for VNZ in healthy subjects.
Study VX18-561-001 showed accumulation ratio of AUCtau ranged 1.2-1.7 for D-IVA in healthy subjects.

Time to achieve
steady-state

Refer to the approved label of TRIKAFTA, the accumulation ratio of TEZ is 2.1 at steady state following 100 mg once daily
dosing regimen.
Study VX17-121-001 Part C showed that following once daily regimen in the context of TC, the VNZ Ctrough value was
stabilized by Day 10 in healthy subjects. Trial VX20-121-102 showed that VNZ Ctrough value was stabilized since Day 15
(i.e., the earliest PK sampling time from this trial) following the to-be-marketed FDC tablet once daily administration in
subjects with CF. Given the effective elimination half-life of 93 hours for VNZ, it is reasonable to conclude that the steady
state of VNZ is reached on Day 10.
Study VX18-561-001 showed that following once daily regimen, D-IVA Ctrough value was stabilized around Days 4-5 in
healthy subjects, Trial VX20-121-102 and 103 showed that D-IVA Ctrough value was stabilized since Day 15 (i.e., the
earliest PK sampling time from these trials) following the to-be-marketed FDC tablet once daily administration in subjects
with CF. Given the effective elimination half-life of 19 hours for D-IVA, it is reasonable to conclude that the steady state of
D-IVA is reached no later than Day 5.

Bridge between
to-be-marketed
and clinical
trial/study
formulations

Study VX17-121-001 Part C showed that following once daily regimen in the context of TC, TEZ Ctrough value was
stabilized on Day 7. Trials VX20-121-102 and VX20-121-103 showed that TEZ Ctrough value was stabilized since Day 15
(i.e., the earliest PK sampling time from these trials) following the to-be-marketed FDC tablet once daily administration in
subjects with CF. Given the elimination half-life of 22 hours for TEZ, it is reasonable to conclude that the steady state of
TEZ is reached no later than Day 7.
To-be-marketed formulation VNZ [Form D]/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet (dosing strength of 10 mg/50 mg/125 mg and
3 mg/20 mg/50 mg) was used in pivotal phase 3 trials and pediatric Trial 105.
During the development, VNZ [Form A] was used in phase 1 and phase 2 studies. Studies VX19-121-003 and VX21-121005 demonstrated that the VNZ Cmax and AUC from Form D (to-be marketed) was approximately 50% of that from Form A.
The results from Studies VX19-121-003 and VX21-121-005 supported the dose selection of 20 mg VNZ [Form D] in phase
3 trials.

Drug Information
Absorption
Absolute bioavailability of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA were not determined.
The median (min, max) of Tmax of VNZ and D-IVA were 10 (4, 12) hours (Study VX17-121-001, Part B) and 4 (3, 6) hours
(Study VX18-561-001), respectively.
In Study VX21-121-005, food effect (high- and low-fat) on VNZ 20 mg/TEZ 100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg FDC tablet was
evaluated. High-fat food increases AUC and Cmax of VNZ by 5.8- and 6.3-fold, TEZ by 1.2- and 1.1-fold, D-IVA by 4.2- and
5.4-fold, respectively. Low-fat food increases AUC and Cmax of VNZ by 3.7- and 3.9-fold, TEZ by 1.1- and 1.2-fold, D-IVA by
3.3- and 3.4-fold, respectively. Food did not relevantly change Tmax of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
Distribution
After a single oral dose of 2 VNZ 10-mg/TEZ 50-mg/D-IVA 125-mg FDC tablets, the mean (SD) apparent volumes of
distribution were 90.4 (31.3) L for VNZ, 123 (43.2) L for TEZ, and 157 (47.3) L for D-IVA.

Plasma protein
Plasma protein binding of VNZ and D-IVA are greater than 99%. Plasma protein binding of TEZ is approximately 99%.
binding
Drug as substrate Based on in vitro data, VNZ is not a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3.
of transporters
Based on in vitro data, D-IVA and M1-D-IVA are substrates of P-gp and BCRP but are not substrates of OATP1B1 or
OATP1B3.
Refer to the approved label of ELX/TEZ/IVA, TEZ is a substrate for P-gp and BCRP, but not a substrate of OATP1B1 or
OATP1B3.
Elimination
Mass balance
Unchanged VNZ accounted for most of the total circulating radioactivity in plasma and there were no major circulating
results
metabolites of VNZ. VNZ was mainly cleared by metabolism in humans. The total excretion (in urine and feces) of the
radiolabeled dose over 408 hours (17 days) was approximately 92.1%, with fecal excretion being the major route of
elimination accounting for 91.6% and urinary excretion accounting for 0.5% (Study VX20-121-004).
Refer to the mass balance Study VX13-661-005, the recovery rate of 14C-labeled TEZ is 85.9% at 21 days post-single dose
with 72.2% and 13.7% excreted in feces and urine, respectively. No unchanged TEZ is detected in urine and unchanged
TEZ only constitute 30.1% of the dose in feces.
A human mass balance trial of D-IVA was not performed. However, it is expected that the excretion pathway for D-IVA is
similar to IVA. Refer to the approved label of IVA (trade name: KALYDECO), following oral administration, the majority of
ivacaftor (87.8%) is eliminated in the feces after metabolic conversion. There was minimal urinary excretion of ivacaftor as
unchanged parent.

Drug Information
Mean (SD) apparent clearance were 1.18 (0.455) L/hr for VNZ, 0.937 (0.338) L/hr for TEZ, and 6.52 (2.77) L/hr for D-IVA
Mean (% CV) of the elimination half-life of VNZ and D-IVA were 50.4 (32.3) hours (Study VX17-121-001, Part B) and 21.8
(28.1) hours (Study VX18-561-001), respectively. The effective half-life of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA are 92.8 (30.2), 22.5 (5.9),
and 19.2 (8.7) hours, respectively.
VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA are primarily metabolized by CYP3A4/5.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Pediatric PK and safety trial (Study VX21-121-105) demonstrated that the exposures (trough concentrations) of each APIs
and their active metabolites in subjects aged 6 through 11 years following the proposed doses (VNZ 20 mg/TEZ 100 mg/DIVA 250 mg for body weight ≥40 kg and VNZ 12 mg/TEZ 60 mg/D-IVA 150 mg for body weight <40 kg) were generally
within the range of adults and adolescents following VNZ 20 mg/TEZ 100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg.
Weight was identified as the main factor contributing to the differences in the PK between adult and pediatric subjects. Age
was not identified as a significant covariate after the body weight adjustment
No dedicated renal impairment study has been conducted for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet. It is reasonable that no dose
adjustment for subjects with mild or moderate renal impairment due to the minimal renal excretion for all three active
ingredients. In addition, popPK showed similar exposure of VNZ/TEZ/F-IVA between healthy subjects and subjects with
mild/moderate renal impairment. Therefore, the proposal for use with caution in people with CF with severe RI or end-stage
renal disease is also reasonable.
Following administration of a single dose of VNZ 10mg/TEZ 50 mg/D-IVA 125 mg in hepatic impairment (HI) Study VX21121-008, the total exposure (AUCinf) of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA decreased 30%, remained comparable, and decreased 20%,
respectively, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment when compared to matched subjects with normal hepatic
function. Similarly, the free drug exposure (AUCu,inf) of VNZ and D-IVA decreased 20% and 40%, respectively, in subjects
with moderate HI when compared to matched subjects with normal hepatic function
Based on these results and hepatotoxicity of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, it is reasonable that no dosage adjustment in patients with
mild hepatic impairment is proposed. It is reasonable that the Applicant proposed VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA use in patients with
moderate HI should only be considered when there is a clear medical need, and the benefit exceeds the risk. If used, no
dose adjustment is recommended. VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA use is not recommended in patients with severe hepatic impairment.

Characteristic
Moderate or
strong CYP3A4
inhibitor

Drug Information
Clinical DDI studies evaluated the effects of the strong CYP3A inhibitor itraconazole on the PK of VNZ, TEZ and D-IVA.
Coadministration with itraconazole increased VNZ AUC by 3.5- to 10.5-fold, TEZ AUC by 4.0- to 4.5-fold and D-IVA AUC by
11-fold.
PBPK analysis was used to predict the interaction effect of moderate CYP3A inhibitors. Simulations indicated that
coadministration with moderate CYP3A inhibitors may increase the AUC of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA by approximately 2.4- to
4-fold, 2-fold, and 3- to 5-fold, respectively.
Simulations indicated that predicted fold changes in AUC with moderate and strong CYP3A inhibitors were similar between
children aged 6 through 11 years and adults at the proposed therapeutic doses of VNZ and D-IVA, assuming the exposure
levels of CYP34 inhibitors are similar between adults and pediatrics.
The dose of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA should be reduced when coadministered with strong and moderate CYP3A inhibitors, as
follows:

Inhibition/induction D-IVA may inhibit CYP2C9 (in vitro IC50 value of 15.6 µM with R value of 1.03). The risk for clinical inhibition was not ruled
of metabolism
out using PBPK analysis. Therefore, monitoring of the international normalized ratio (INR) during coadministration of

In vitro results showed that TEZ has low potential to inhibit P-gp, BCRP, OCT2, OAT1, or OAT3.

Clinical impact

In vitro results showed similar P-gp and BCRP inhibition potencies between IVA and D-IVA. Study VX14-661-006 showed
that coadministration of ivacaftor or tezacaftor/ivacaftor with digoxin, a sensitive P-gp substrate, increased digoxin AUC by
1.3-fold, consistent with weak inhibition of P-gp by ivacaftor. Therefore, the review team agree that a similar P-gp DDI-related
language can applied from IVA to D-IVA (i.e., when used concomitantly with digoxin or other substrates of P gp with a narrow
therapeutic index such as cyclosporine, everolimus, sirolimus, and tacrolimus, caution and appropriate monitoring should be
used).

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
The proposed dose of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC product is VNZ [Form D] 20 mg/TEZ
100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg once daily (QD) for subjects 12 years of age or older and subjects 6
through 11 years with body weight ≥40 kg; and VNZ [Form D] 12 mg/TEZ 60 mg/D-IVA
150 mg QD for subjects 6 through 11 years with body weight <40 kg.
The dose selection of VNZ and D-IVA for phase 3 trials were primarily determined based on the
two phase 2 dose-ranging studies (Study VX18-121-101 and Study VX18-561-101 for VNZ and
D-IVA, respectively), and two phase 1 relative bioavailability studies (Study VX19-121-003 and
Study VX21-121-005). The dose selection of TEZ is based on the approved TEZ dose of
ELX/TEZ/IVA (Trade name: TRIKAFTA, NDA 212273 and NDA 217660) for the treatment of
CF.
The proposed pediatric dose for subjects 6 through 11 years of age was primarily supported by
Trial VX22-121-105 and population PK modeling and simulations.

6.1.1. Dose Selection of Vanzacaftor
Dose-ranging results from part 1 of the phase 2 study, StudyVX18-121-101, demonstrated a
numerically higher percent predicted forced expiratory volume in 1 second (ppFEV1)
improvement from baseline on Day 29 following VNZ [Form A] 10 mg QD when compared to
other VNZ doses in the context of triple combination (TC) with TEZ 100 mg QD and IVA
150 mg every 12 hours (q12h) in CF subjects with F508del /minimal function (MF) variants. In
addition, a near maximal response on sweat chloride (SwCl) reduction was observed between
10 mg [Form A] and 20 mg [Form A]. Therefore, a dose of 10 mg VNZ [Form A] QD was
(b) (4)
selected for further evaluation in phase 3 studies. However,
Additional PK bridging studies (Study VX19-121-003 and Study VX21-121-005) were
conducted between the two forms, and the results showed that the bioavailability of VNZ
[Form D] was only about 50% of that of VNZ [Form A] following the same dose administration.
Based on these results, a dose of 20 mg VNZ [Form D] QD was eventually selected and carried
into phase 3 trials.
VNZ Dose-Ranging Study VX18-121-101 (Study 101)
Study 101 provided primary support for VNZ dose selection for the pivotal phase 3 studies.
Study 101 was a 2-part, randomized, double-blind, placebo- (Part 1) or TEZ/IVA-controlled
(Part 2), proof-of-concept trial of VNZ in CF subjects. VNZ [Form A] was used in both Part 1
and Part 2 of this trial.

31
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
The primary endpoint was the absolute change in ppFEV1 through Day 29. Key secondary
endpoints were the absolute change from baseline in SwCl and the absolute change from baseline
in cystic fibrosis questionnaire-revised (CFQ-R) respiratory domain (RD) score at Day 29.
In Part 1, CF subjects with F508del /MF variants were enrolled. The treatment groups were VNZ
[Form A] 5, 10, and 20 mg QD in the context of TC with TEZ 100 mg QD and IVA 150 mg
q12h in comparison to triple-placebo for 4 weeks. Subjects with no previous treatment with
medications that affect CFTR processing or function within 28 days before the first dose were
randomized in a 2:2:1:1 ratio to the TC-VNZ 20 mg, TC-VNZ 10 mg, TC-VNZ 5 mg, and
placebo arms. In total, 58 subjects were randomized and received at least 1 dose of trial drug. In
Part 2, CF subjects with F508del/F508del variant with no previous treatment with a medication
that affects CFTR processing or function within 28 days before the start of TEZ/IVA run-in
period (4 weeks) were enrolled. VNZ [Form A] 20 mg QD/TEZ 100 mg QD/IVA 150 mg q12h
was evaluated in comparison to TEZ 100 mg QD/IVA 150 mg q12h. Subjects were randomized
in a 2:1 ratio to the TC-VNZ 20 mg and TEZ/IVA arms. Twenty-nine (29) subjects were
enrolled and 28 completed the treatment.
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment for 4 weeks in CF subjects with F508del/MF or F508del/F508del
genotypes resulted in improvements in ppFEV1, SwCl, and CFQ-R RD score (secondary efficacy
endpoints) from the respective baseline when compared to the control groups (placebo for Part 1
and TEZ/IVA for Part 2) as presented in Table 10. In Part 1, among three doses of VNZ [Form
A], the 10 mg dose showed the numerically greatest improvement in ppFEV1 response compared
to placebo group, versus VNZ 5 mg and 20 mg.

VNZ Formulation Bridging Study VX19-121-003 (Study 003)
VNZ dose selection was also based on an assessment of the relative bioavailability of VNZ
[Form A] physical formulation compared to VNZ [Form D] physical formulation in Study 003.
Study 003 was a phase 1, open-label, randomized, 2-sequence, 2-period crossover study to
evaluate the relative bioavailability of a single dose FDC tablet of VNZ [Form D] 20 mg/TEZ
100 mg/D-IVA 150 mg (test) compared to single dose VNZ [Form A] 20 mg, TEZ 100 mg, and
D-IVA 150 mg coadministered as separate tablets (reference) under fed conditions.
Twenty healthy subjects were randomized and received at least one dose of study drug. The PK
metrics and relative bioavailability of VNZ (Form A versus Form D), TEZ, and D-IVA after
single-dose administration as an FDC tablet or as separate tablets was statistically evaluated. The
geometric least squares mean ratios and their associated 90% CIs are provided for exposure of
each active pharmaceutical ingredient (API) in Table 11. The exposure (AUC and Cmax) of VNZ
following Form A was approximately 50% of the exposure following FDC tablet, which
contained VNZ Form D. Based on these results, a dose of VNZ [Form D] 20 mg QD provides
similar exposure as the VNZ [Form A] 10 mg QD dose, which was evaluated in VNZ doseranging trial, Study 101.

6.1.2. Dose Selection of Deutivacaftor
The dose of D-IVA 250 mg QD was selected for the phase 3 trials in subjects ≥12 years of age
because this dose resulted in numerically greater improvements in SwCl response with similar
ppFEV1 response when compared to D-IVA 150 mg QD in the dose-ranging study, Study VX18561-101.
Study VX18-561-101 (Study 561-101)
Study 561-101 was a phase 2, randomized, double-blind, parallel-group, and active-controlled
study of D-IVA monotherapy in CF subjects who have a gating variant and were previously
taking a stable dose of IVA. Four dose levels of D-IVA (25, 50, 150, and 250 mg) were
evaluated to provide a broad range of exposure for exposure-response analyses.
The primary endpoint was the absolute change in ppFEV1 at Week 12. Key secondary endpoint
was the absolute change from baseline in SwCl at Week 12.
In total, 77 subjects who were on stable IVA treatment were randomized and 75 received at least
one dose of study drug. Two lower dose treatment groups (25 mg and 50 mg) were prematurely
discontinued because of high drop-off rate, which was presumably related to insufficient CFTR
modulation Results from Study 561-101 demonstrated treatment with either D-IVA 250 mg QD
or D-IVA 150 mg QD resulted in similar absolute values of ppFEV1 change from baseline
compared to IVA 150 mg q12h treatment (Table 13). However, treatment with D-IVA 250 mg
QD resulted in numerically greater improvements in SwCl response compared to D-IVA 150 mg
QD. Therefore, the Applicant’s choice of D-IVA 250 mg QD for further evaluation in phase 3
trials was reasonable.

The clinical pharmacology review team acknowledges that Study VX21-121-006 demonstrated
D-IVA Cmax and AUC increased 18% and 27%, respectively, when D-IVA was administered in
TC with VNZ/TEZ compared to D-IVA administered alone. There is a concern whether this

increase of D-IVA systemic exposure of up to 27% may change the efficacy/safety profile of

increase of systemic exposure would contribute additional efficacy of D-IVA in the context of

6.1.3. Dose Selection for Pediatric Subjects 6 Through
11 Years Old
Trial VX21-121-105 (Trial 105)
Trial 105 was an open-label, 2-part (Parts A and B), multicohort (Cohorts 1, 2, and 3),
multicenter trial evaluating the PK, safety, tolerability, and efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA therapy
in CF subjects 1 through 11 years of age who carry at least one triple combination responsive
(TCR) CFTR variant. Cohorts A1 and B1 evaluated subjects 6 through 11 years of age.
The primary objective of Trial 105 was to evaluate the PK, safety, and tolerability of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and relevant metabolites when dosed in TC in pediatric CF subjects.
Subjects in Cohort A1 received VNZ 10 mg QD/TEZ 50 mg QD/D-IVA 125 mg QD for 22 days.
Subjects who participated in Cohort A1 were permitted to participate in Cohort B1 if they met
eligibility criteria (refer to Section 15 for complete enrollment criteria). A review of safety,
tolerability, and available PK data was completed by an internal team after Cohort A1 completed
treatment to determine the doses and weight cutoffs for Cohort B1.
In Cohort A1, 17 subjects 6 through 11 years of age were randomized and received at least one
dose of trial drug. All subjects received VNZ 10 mg/TEZ 50 mg/D-IVA 125 mg QD, which is
half the dose for CF subjects ≥12 years of age. The comparison results showed that the mean
plasma concentration observed at the end of dosing interval (Ctrough) values in Cohort A1 (on Day
22) of three active ingredients and their active metabolites in subjects 6 through 11 years of age
were generally lower than that from adults and adolescents in the pivotal phase 3 Trials 102 and
103 as presented in Table 14.
Therefore, the pediatric VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA doses were increased in both body weight tiers in
Cohort B1 with the same adult/adolescent dose for subjects ≥40 kg and VNZ 12 mg/TEZ
60 mg/D-IVA 150 mg for subjects <40 kg. Following the new dosing algorithm, overall trough
concentrations were within the range for each of the analytes previously observed in CF subjects
≥12 years of age in pivotal phase 3 Trials 102 and 103.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Results of Pooled Analyses
The evaluation of efficacy was based on the analyses of the individual trials. Pooled efficacy
analyses will not be discussed in this review.

6.2.2. Trial VX20-121-102 (Trial 102)
6.2.2.1. Design, Trial 102
Trial VX20-121-102 (Trial 102) was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, parallelgroup, ELX/TEZ/IVA-controlled, noninferiority trial evaluating the safety and efficacy of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in subjects with CF 12 years of age and older. The trial included a 4-week
run-in period during which all subjects received ELX/TEZ/IVA to establish a reliable ontreatment (ELX/TEZ/IVA) baseline for the treatment period. Subjects were then randomized 1:1
to receive VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA or ELX/TEZ/IVA for a 52-week treatment period. Randomization
was stratified by: age at screening, ppFEV1 during the run-in period (Day -14 clinic assessment),
SwCl during the run-in period (Day -14 clinic assessment), and prior use of ELX/TEZ/IVA or
medications that contain one or more of its components. The full review of the trial protocol
design, subject population, objectives, and endpoints is in Section 15.1.
The dosages evaluated in the phase 3 trials were:
•

Active control: ELX 200 mg QD/TEZ 100 mg QD/IVA 150 mg q12h

•

Treatment: VNZ 20 mg QD/TEZ 100 mg QD/D-IVA 250 mg QD

The primary objective of Trial 102 was to evaluate the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in
adolescent and adult CF subjects. The efficacy endpoints are listed below:
Primary Endpoint
•

Absolute change from baseline in ppFEV1 through Week 24

Key Secondary Endpoints
•

Absolute change from baseline in sweat chloride (SwCl) through Week 24

•

Proportion of subjects with SwCl <60mmol/L through Week 24 (pooled with data from
Trials 102 and 103)

•

Proportion of subjects with SwCl <30mmol/L through Week 24 (pooled with data from
Trials 102 and 103)

39
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Other Secondary Endpoints
•

Number of CF pulmonary exacerbations (PEx) through Week 52

•

Absolute change from baseline in CFQ-R RD score through Week 24

•

Absolute change from baseline in ppFEV1 through Week 52

•

Absolute change from baseline in SwCl through Week 52

•

Proportion of subjects with SwCl <60mmol/L through Week 24

•

Proportion of subjects with SwCl <30mmol/L through Week 24

•

Safety and tolerability assessments based on adverse events (AEs), clinical laboratory values,
electrocardiograms, vital signs, and pulse oximetry

ELX/TEZ/IVA and other medications that affect CFTR processing or function have contributed
to significant clinical benefits in the CF population and are currently used by over 80% of
patients in the United States (CFF 2023). The Applicant designed a noninferiority (NI) trial with
ELX/TEZ/IVA as the active control. Given the magnitude of treatment effect and pervasive use
of ELX/TEZ/IVA as the standard of care, the Applicant argued use of placebo control would
have been unethical and infeasible, with which the Division agreed.
There was not a previously accepted NI margin: i.e., an amount of loss of pulmonary function, as
measured by forced expiratory volume in one second (FEV1) (or any other lung function
measure), by which a new drug could be worse than a reference drug with established efficacy to
determine noninferiority in CF. The Applicant proposed an NI margin for absolute change in
ppFEV1 of -3.0% supported by data from the ELX/TEZ/IVA development program, which
included the following rationale:
•

For the population eligible for Trial 102 (F508del/MF), the lower bound of the 95% CI of the
treatment effect for ELX/TEZ/IVA was approximately 12% for the ppFEV1. According to
the FDA guidance for industry Non-Inferiority Clinical Trials to Establish Effectiveness
(November 2016), preservation of at least 50% of the conservative effect of 12% (M1) is
recommended as a clinically acceptable NI margin (M2), i.e., ≤6% (equal to 50% of 12%
effect size). Thus, the Applicant proposed 3.0% as the NI margin, which the Division agreed
is reasonable. Refer to Section 15 for additional details on the NI margin.

All other efficacy endpoints were analyzed using superiority comparisons, some of which were
controlled for multiplicity (refer to Section 15.1 for details). As reviewed in Section 6.3.2, the
key secondary endpoints were limited to SwCl-based endpoints and were the only secondary
endpoints analyzed under multiplicity control. The Division views SwCl as an important
pharmacodynamic marker of CFTR function but advised the Applicant to consider additional
non-SwCl key secondary endpoint(s) that represent clinically meaningful outcomes (e.g., PEx,
CFQ-R). The Applicant included assessments of these clinically meaningful endpoints, but they
were maintained as ‘other’ secondary endpoints and not included in the statistical analysis
hierarchy. The results of these other endpoints are reviewed in more detail in Section 6.2.2.4 and
Section 16.2.

40
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Overall, the trial design, endpoint selection, and endpoint classifications were reasonable to
demonstrate noninferiority in a CF population with respect to ppFEV1.
6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 102
The eligibility criteria for Trial 102 were appropriate for capturing a population of adults and
adolescents with stable CF. The CFTR variant-based eligibility for Trial 102 included subjects
heterozygous for the F508del and minimal function (MF) CFTR variants, defined as either a
variant that results in no translated CFTR protein or a variant that is nonresponsive to TEZ, IVA,
or TEZ/IVA based on in vitro testing.
The key inclusion criteria included:
•

Ages 12 years and older

•

Heterozygous F508del and a MF CFTR variant

•

Percent predicted FEV1 (Quanjer et al. 2012):
– Subjects receiving ELX/TEZ/IVA at screening: ≥40% and ≤90%
– Subjects not receiving ELX/TEZ/IVA at screening: ≥40% and ≤80%

The key exclusion criteria included:
•

History of intolerance to trial drug including ELX/TEZ/IVA

•

History of comorbidity including but not limited to hepatic cirrhosis, moderate to severe
hepatic impairment, solid organ or hematological transplantation

•

Acute upper or lower respiratory tract infection, infectious PEx of CF, or changes in therapy
(including antibiotics) for sinopulmonary disease

The trial population was consistent with similar clinical development programs in CF, including
Trial VX17-445-102 used to support the registration of ELX/TEZ/IVA. These similarities in the
population further support the use of a noninferiority trial design with ELX/TEZ/IVA as the
active control. The exclusion of subjects unable to tolerate ELX/TEZ/IVA is reasonable and
appropriate given the design of the trial; however, this is a significant limitation that affects the
generalizability of safety results. Additional information regarding the trial’s population is
included in Section 15.
6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 102
The primary efficacy analysis compared VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA using a mixedeffects model for repeated measures (MMRM) adjusted for treatment, visit, age at screening
(<18 versus ≥18 years of age), and treatment-by-visit interaction, with baseline ppFEV1 and
baseline SwCl as continuous covariates. The primary result obtained from the model was the
estimated treatment difference through Week 24, which was estimated by averaging Weeks 16
and 24. The adjusted mean difference with a 2-sided 95% CI and the 1-sided P value for NI were
provided. The primary null hypothesis was to be rejected, and noninferiority demonstrated, if the
41
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
lower bound of the 95% CI was ≥­3.0%. Intercurrent events of premature treatment
discontinuation and use of non-trial medications that affect CFTR processing or function for >3
days were handled with a treatment policy strategy. Conditional on the observed data and
covariates, missing data was assumed to be missing at random, and its impact was assessed by
tipping point analyses. The analysis was conducted on the full analysis set (FAS) population,
defined as all randomized subjects who carry the intended CFTR genotype and received at least
one dose of trial drug.
To address multiplicity in the analysis of the efficacy endpoints, the Applicant pre-specified a
hierarchical order for the primary endpoint and key secondary endpoints of absolute change from
baseline in SwCl through Week 24, and proportion of subjects with SwCl <60mmol/L and SwCl
<30mmol/L through Week 24 on the pooled with data from Trials 102 and 103. See Section 15.1
for additional information.
6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 102
Subject Enrollment and Disposition
A total of 435 subjects were enrolled in Trial 102. During the run-in period, 37 (8.5%) of
subjects discontinued the trial and did not enter the treatment period. The most common reason
reported for discontinuation (22, 5.1%) during the run-in period was an adverse event of which
the majority were related to CF pulmonary exacerbations. Following the run-in period, 405
subjects were randomized and 398 (98.3%) received at least 1 dose of the trial drug and are
considered the FAS. A summary of the subject enrollment and disposition is included in Table
15.

The rates of trial discontinuation were also balanced between groups, and the main reasons cited

subjects in VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA group) and adverse events (4 [2.0%] subjects in ELX/TEZ/IVA
group and 4 [2.0%] subjects in VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA group). The rates of treatment and trial
discontinuation were low. Rates of trial completion and treatment discontinuation in Trial 102
were similar to other phase 3 programs in this population.
Subject Demographics
The population enrolled in Trial 102 had a mean age of 30.8 years (range 12.2 to 71.6 years),
was 59.0% male, and 14.3% adolescents (12 to <18 years). The mean age of Trial 102 was older
than phase 3 trials from the ELX/TEZ/IVA program, but not unreasonable based on the known
demographics of the CF population. The baseline subject demographics are shown in Table 16.

Overall, the baseline CF disease characteristics were balanced between the treatment groups and
reasonable for a stable adolescent and adult CF population. The overall use of other CF
background symptomatic therapies was lower than the phase 3 trials for ELX/TEZ/IVA, with the
exception of inhaled hypertonic saline and inhaled bronchodilators. This difference is not
unexpected and is likely reflective of changes in prescribing practices over time in the context of
ELX/TEZ/IVA availability. The rate of positive Pseudomonas aeruginosa culture within the last
2 years was also lower than the phase 3 trials of ELX/TEZ/IVA and is consistent with recently
published data about the overall CF population (CFF 2023). These differences in baseline disease
characteristics are not significant enough to affect the appropriateness of the reference trials for
the noninferiority comparison.
Trial 102 limited enrollment to subjects who were heterozygous for F508del and an MF variant.
The past medical history and concomitant medications were reviewed and are consistent with a
CF adult and adolescent population and balanced between treatment groups. Additional
information regarding subject genotype, past medical history, and concomitant medications is
included in Section 16.1.1.
Analysis of Efficacy – Primary Endpoint
Trial 102 was designed as a noninferiority trial with a designated margin of -3.0% for the
primary endpoint of ppFEV1. The least squares (LS) mean difference for change from baseline in
ppFEV1 through Week 24 was 0.2% (95% CI: -0.7%, 1.1%) in VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared to
ELX/TEZ/IVA based on the FAS population, as shown in Table 18. Since the lower bound of the
95% CI was greater than -3.0%, VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was demonstrated to be noninferior to
ELX/TEZ/IVA in ppFEV1.

Abbreviations: CFB, change from baseline; CI, confidence interval; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elexacaftor; FAS, full analysis set;

The Applicant specified that the primary endpoint would be analyzed through Week 24, defined
as the average of the values from Weeks 16 and 24. During the development of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, the Division recommended the use of a landmark analysis at Week 24 to
better capture durability of treatment effect. The Applicant maintained the through 24-week
analysis; therefore, the statistical reviewer and analyst performed an additional landmark analysis
using an MMRM analysis for change from baseline in ppFEV1 at Week 24. The landmark
analysis at Week 24 resulted in an LS mean difference (95% CI) of 0.3% (-1.3%, 0.8%), which
supported the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA, as well. In addition, the
robustness of the analysis results regarding missing data was supported by the tipping point
sensitivity analysis. See Section 16.2 for details.
Although the landmark analysis was post hoc and exploratory, the results were supportive of the
treatment effect of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. For future development programs, the Division continues
to recommend landmark analyses for ppFEV1 endpoints.

secondary endpoints appear supportive of efficacy for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in patients with CF.
Efficacy Subgroup Analyses
The Applicant performed multiple subgroup analyses for the primary endpoint, including age,
ppFEV1 at baseline, SwCl at baseline, sex, and geographic region. Overall, the subgroups
evaluations were generally consistent with the overall population. Select subgroup analyses are
summarized in Figure 2.

For the adolescent subgroup (subjects with an age at screening of <18 years), the LS mean

results in the adolescent subgroup is limited by the wide 95% CI, likely because of the small
sample size.
In subjects with a SwCl of <30mmol/L at baseline, the 95% confidence interval of the LS mean
difference in ppFEV1 between the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment groups ruled
out the NI margin and excluded the null. However, the interpretation of this finding is limited by
a small sample size, as it represents less than 10% of the overall population; therefore, the
clinical meaning of this finding is unclear.

6.2.3. Trial VX20-121-103 (Trial 103)
6.2.3.1. Design, Trial 103
The overall design and endpoint selection for Trial 103 were the same as Trial 102 (reviewed in
Section 6.2.2.1 and Section 15.1). The dosages evaluated in Trial 103 were also the same as Trial
102. The Applicant proposed a noninferiority margin of absolute change in FEV1 of -3.0%
supported by data from the ELX/TEZ/IVA development program that included a similar
rationale as for Trial 102:
48
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
•

For the population eligible for Trial 103 (F/F and others), there were no historical placebocontrolled trials to derive a NI margin by the usual approach. The Applicant employed a
novel method based on Bucher’s network indirect comparison approach to come up with
12% as M1 (Bucher et al. 1997). Then similarly, the Applicant proposed 3.0% as the NI
margin, and the Division generally agreed with the proposal, acknowledging the lack of
historical placebo-controlled data. See Section 15.1 for additional information on the NI
margin.

As in Trial 102, all other efficacy endpoints were analyzed using superiority comparisons, some
of which were controlled for multiplicity (refer to Section 15.1 for details).
6.2.3.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 103
The eligibility criteria for Trial 103 were also very similar to Trial 102 (reviewed in
Section 6.2.2.1 and Section 15.1) with the exception of the CFTR variant based inclusion criteria.
Trial 103 enrolled subjects with the following CFTR genotypes:
•

Homozygous for F508del (F/F)

•

Heterozygous for F508del and a gating variant (F/G)

•

Heterozygous for F508del and a residual function variant (F/RF)

•

At least one other TCR CFTR variant identified as responsive to ELZ/TEZ/IVA and no
F508del variant (TCR/non-F)

G variants and RF variants, also referred to as conduction variants, are classified as pathologic
CFTR variants in which CFTR protein is created and trafficked to the cell surface but chloride
transport through the protein channel is decreased because the channel does not open or function
properly (CFF 2024). A summary of the G and RF variants eligible for Trial 102 provided by the
Applicant is included in Section 15.1. Note there can be overlap of certain variants between these
two CFTR classification groups.
6.2.3.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 103
The statistical analysis plan for Trial 103 was the same as Trial 102 (reviewed in Section 6.2.2.3
and Section 15.1), with the exception of sample size that was powered at 95% for Trial 103
compared to 90% for Trial 102.
6.2.3.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 103
Subject Enrollment and Disposition
A total of 597 subjects were enrolled in Trial 103. During the run-in period, 24 (4.0%) subjects
discontinued the trial. The primary reason listed for trial discontinuation was “other” (11
subjects, 1.8%) and included reasons such as subject preference and antibiotic requirements for
CF pulmonary exacerbations. Following the run-in period, 574 subjects were randomized and

reasonable. Any imbalances in the subject demographics between the treatment groups were
minor and unlikely to affect the overall analysis.
The enrolled population had an average ppFEV1 of 66.8% and an average SwCl of 42.8mmol/L.
Similar to Trial 102, the majority of subjects were previously prescribed drugs that affect CFTR
processing or function (491, 85.7%) the most common of which was ELX/TEZ/IVA (389,
67.9%). The baseline average SwCl in Trial 103 is lower than in Trial 102, which is reflective of
the differences in CFTR variant eligibility criteria. The baseline CF disease characteristics are
reviewed in Table 23.

Analysis of Efficacy – Primary Endpoint
Trial 103 was also designed as noninferiority trial with a designated margin of -3.0%. Through
Week 24, treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was also noninferior to ELX/TEZ/IVA in the
absolute change from baseline in ppFEV1, the primary efficacy endpoint. The LS mean
difference was 0.2% (95% CI: -0.5%, 0.9%) in the FAS population, as shown in Table 26.

Similar to Trial 102, the Applicant specified that the primary endpoint would be analyzed
through Week 24, defined as the average of the values from Week 16 and 24. The statistical
reviewer and analyst preformed a landmark analysis at Week 24 that resulted in an LS mean
difference (95% CI) of 0.2% (-0.6%, 1.0%). Although the landmark analysis was post hoc and
exploratory, the results were supportive of the treatment effect of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The
robustness of the analysis result regarding missing data was supported by the tipping point
sensitivity analysis. See Section 16.2 for additional information.

subgroups evaluations were generally consistent with the overall population. Select subgroup

the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.

6.2.4. Trial VX21-121-105 (Trial 105)
6.2.4.1. Design, Trial 105 (Cohorts A1 and B1)
Trial 105 was a phase 3, 2-part (Parts A and B), multicenter, multicohort, open-label trial in
pediatric CF subjects. Cohorts A1 and B1 enrolled subjects 6 through 11 years of age and the
final analysis results are presented below. The remainder of the cohorts in Trial 105 are either
ongoing or have not yet begun enrollment. Ultimately, Trial 105 will enroll CF subjects 1
through 11 years of age (inclusive).
Part A (PK)
There was a 28-day screening period followed by a 15-day treatment period. A review of safety,
tolerability, and available PK data was completed by the Applicant after Cohort A1 completed
treatment to determine the doses and weight cutoffs for Cohort B1.

57
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Primary Endpoints
•

PK parameters of VNZ, TEZ, D-IVA, and relevant metabolites

•

Safety and tolerability as determined by AEs, clinical laboratory values, standard 12-lead
electrocardiograms, vital signs, and pulse oximetry

All subjects in Cohort A1 received VNZ 10 mg QD/TEZ 50 mg QD/D-IVA 125 mg QD.
Part B (Safety and Tolerability)
Part B also included a 28-day screening period with a 28-day ELX/TEZ/IVA run-in period to
establish an on-treatment (ELX/TEZ/IVA) baseline. The run-in period was waved for subjects
already receiving stable ELX/TEZ/IVA prior to enrollment. The screening and run-in (if
applicable) periods were followed by a 24 Week treatment period, during which all subjects
received VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
The primary endpoint of Part B was safety and tolerability of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Additional
efficacy endpoints from Trials 102 and 103 were examined in exploratory analyses. A full
review of the trial protocol design, trial population, objectives, and endpoints is included in
Section 15.2. In general, the trial design and population were consistent with previous trials in
the CF pediatric population for other similar medications.
The doses of trial drug for Cohort B1 are reviewed in Table 29.

6.2.4.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 105
The eligibility criteria for Cohorts A1 and B1 of Trial 105 were reasonable for a stable CF
population aged 6 through 11 years. The CFTR-variant based eligibility included at least one
TCR variant, including F508del, in the CFTR gene.

58
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
The key inclusion criteria included:
•

Ages 6 through 11 years (inclusive) on the date of informed consent

•

Weight between 5th and 9th percentile (inclusive) for weight-for-age at screening

•

At least one TCR CFTR variant, including F508del

•

FEV1≥60% predicted at screening

•

Stable CF disease judged by the investigator

The key exclusion criteria included:
•

History of intolerance to trial drug including ELX/TEZ/IVA

•

History of comorbidity including but not limited to hepatic cirrhosis, moderate to severe
hepatic impairment, chronic kidney disease, solid organ, or hematological transplantation

•

Acute upper or lower respiratory tract infection, infectious PEx of CF, or changes in therapy
(including antibiotics) for sinopulmonary disease

The trial population in Cohorts A1 and B1 of Trial 105 were reasonable and similar to other
pediatric CF development programs.
6.2.4.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 105
Since Cohort A1 was for PK assessment, the statistical analysis plan is described for Cohort B1.
For change from baseline endpoints, a MMRM approach was employed. The primary result
obtained from the model was the average treatment effect through Week 24. The estimated mean
change from baseline through Week 24 along with the corresponding 2-sided 95% CI were
provided. For the number of events variables, a descriptive summary along with the observed
event rate was provided. The number and proportion of subjects with SwCl <60mmol/L was
descriptively summarized with the 2-sided 95% CI based on Clopper-Pearson method.
6.2.4.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 105
Subject Enrollment and Disposition
In Trial 105, Cohort A1 enrolled 17 subjects and all subjects completed treatment and the trial
cohort. Cohort B1 enrolled 78 subjects: 17 (21.8%) entered the treatment period after completion
of the ELX/TEZ/IVA run-in period, and the remaining 61 (78.2%) subjects directly entered the
treatment period following screening, since they were on stable commercial ELX/TEZ/IVA for
at least 28 days prior to screening.
Cohort B1 enrolled 78 subjects and all subjects completed the trial. The majority of subjects also
completed treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA with the exception of 1 (1.3%) subject who
discontinued treatment due to an adverse event (reviewed in Section 7.6.2.4). The rates of
completion of the trial and treatment were consistent with other similarly designed trials in this

population. The majority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA exposure occurred in Cohort B1, and this will be
the focus of the remainder of this section of the review unless otherwise noted.

6.2.4.5. Pediatric (6 Through 11 Years of Age) Efficacy
Summary
The PK data for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA administration, reviewed in Section 14.2.6, demonstrated that
systemic exposures were comparable to those observed in the adolescent and adult population.
Because of the similar systemic exposures and because the disease process in the older
population is the same as that in the 6- to 11-year-old population, efficacy in this age group was
extrapolated from the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA adolescent and adult development program.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Satisfaction of 21 CFR 300.50, the “Combination
Rule”
Issue
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is a FDC drug product. The Applicant has proposed to use a combination of
clinical and in vitro data to support satisfaction of 21 CFR 300.50, i.e., to demonstrate the
contribution of each active ingredient to the claimed effects of the combination product.
Background
The regulatory evidentiary standard to support approval for a new fixed drug combination
product must consider 21 CFR 300.50(a), often referred to as the “combination rule,” which
states that, “two or more drugs may be combined in a single dosage form when each component
makes a contribution to the claimed effect and the dosage of each…is such that the combination
is safe and effective for a significant patient population requiring such concurrent therapy…”.
63
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Often, the Agency relies on factorial design clinical trials to evaluate and support the
contribution of each component in the combination product. However, there is no single
regulatory framework for satisfying the combination rule, and the Agency may exercise
flexibility, particularly in cases when the beneficial treatment effect of a particular drug class is
well understood and established.
During the development of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, the Applicant proposed to leverage a combination
of in vitro and clinical data to demonstrate the individual contributions of VNZ, TEZ, and DIVA to the efficacy of the FDC product, rather than rely on a full factorial design approach. The
Division agreed this approach was reasonable. There is regulatory precedent for this approach,
which is similar to that used to support the approval of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
Assessment
The Division completed a multidisciplinary review of the clinical and in vitro data to evaluate
the contributions of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA, which included the various types of evidence
described below. For additional details of the clinical pharmacology assessment, refer to
Section 14.
The Mechanism of Action or Action Site
•

VNZ and TEZ both facilitate the cellular processing and trafficking of select CFTR variants
(including F508del-CFTR). The binding sites of VNZ and TEZ on the CFTR protein are
different, and in vitro pharmacology results demonstrate that VNZ and TEZ work
synergistically.

•

Similar to IVA, D-IVA is a CFTR potentiator that increases the CFTR channel open
probability at the cell surface and facilitates increased chloride ion transport.

APPEARS THIS WAY ON
ORIGINAL

64
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
The Contribution of D-IVA in the Treatment of CF
•

The D-IVA dose-ranging trial (Study VX18-561-101) demonstrated that D-IVA 150 mg or
250 mg QD resulted in a sustained ppFEV1 response over 12 weeks in CF subjects with
gating CFTR variants who were responsive to and prescribed a stable dose of IVA
monotherapy. In addition, the ppFEV1 change from baseline values following 12-week
treatment with D-IVA 150 mg or 250 mg QD were comparable to CF subjects who were
treated with IVA monotherapy for 12 weeks. The results indicate the similar treatment effect
between D-IVA and IVA on a clinically meaningful endpoint.

•

The following in vitro electrophysiology studies demonstrated the similar CFTR potentiating
potencies between D-IVA and IVA:
– Study PH-088 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have similar effects in increasing
G551D/F508del CFTR chloride transport in HBE cells.
– Study PH-045 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have similar effects in increasing
G551D CFTR chloride transport in FRT cells.
– Study PH-043 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have similar effects in increasing
F508del/F508del CFTR chloride transport in HBE cells.
– Study Q-142 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have almost identical concentrationresponse relationship to increase F508del/F508del and F508del/minimal function (MF)
CFTR chloride transport when in combination with IVA and TEZ in HBE cells.

The Contribution of TEZ in the Treatment of CF
•

In the development program of TEZ/IVA (NDA 210491), Trial VX14-661-108 demonstrated
that TEZ 100 mg QD/IVA 150 mg q12h was superior in improving ppFEV1 from baseline
compared to IVA 150 mg q12h monotherapy in CF subjects with F508del/residual function
variants. The results indicate the contribution of TEZ in treatment of CF on IVA background
therapy on a clinically meaningful endpoint.

•

The following in vitro electrophysiology studies demonstrated the contribution of TEZ in
increasing CFTR chloride transport:
– Factorial Study N035 demonstrated synergistic effect between TEZ and IVA where a
greater F508del/F508del CFTR chloride transport was observed with TEZ/IVA
compared to TEZ or IVA alone in HBE cells. In addition, both TEZ or IVA alone
increased chloride transport when compared to vehicle control.
– Study S525 demonstrated an increase of F508del/F508del and F508del/MF variant
CFTR chloride transport with TEZ/VNZ in HBE cells compared to VNZ alone,
indicating the additional contribution of TEZ on background VNZ treatment.
