Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
The Contribution of VNZ in the Treatment of CF
•

In the development program of ELX/TEZ/IVA (NDA 212273), the phase 3 trial (VX17-445103) demonstrated that ELX/TEZ/IVA is superior compared to TEZ/IVA with respect to the
absolute change from baseline in ppFEV1 at Week 4 in F508del homozygous subjects. This
supports the contribution of ELX to the full efficacy of ELX/TEZ/IVA.

•

In VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA program, Trials 102 and 103 demonstrated noninferiority of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA as measured by the absolute change from baseline in
ppFEV1 through Week 24. The results from these trials, taken together, indicate the necessity
of VNZ to contribute to the full efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA because the dose of TEZ is the
same between VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA and because D-IVA 250 mg QD and
IVA 150 mg q12h have a similar treatment effect (see above).

•

The following in vitro electrophysiology studies demonstrated the contribution of VNZ in the
increase in CFTR chloride transport:
– Study S525 demonstrated a concentration-response relationship for VNZ to increase
F508del/F508del and F508del/MF variant CFTR chloride transport in HBE cells.
– Study S525 demonstrated an increase of F508del/F508del and F508del/MF variant
CFTR chloride transport with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared to TEZ/IVA in HBE cells.
– Study U020 demonstrated that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA have similar effects
increasing N1303K/N1303K and N1303K/minimal function variant CFTR chloride
transport in HBE cells, and both triple combinations are superior to effect of TEZ/IVA.

Conclusion
The following data sources demonstrate the contribution of each component of VNZ/TEZ/DIVA to the effect of the combination product: the demonstration of noninferiority of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA in two phase 3 clinical trials; the available historical clinical
trial data with IVA, TEZ/IVA, and ELX/TEZ/IVA; the support of electrophysiology in vitro
data. The Division concludes that there is adequate data from the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development
program to satisfy the combination rule (21 CFR 300.50).

6.3.2. Clinical Relevance of Sweat Chloride
Concentration Efficacy Endpoints
Issue
SwCl concentrations are pharmacodynamic measurements of CFTR function. The phase 3
development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA included secondary endpoints related to SwCl,
including the change from baseline in SwCl and responder analyses for the proportion of subjects
with SwCl below 60mmol/L and 30mmol/L, which are used clinically as the CF diagnostic
threshold and the normal range threshold, respectively (Farrell et al. 2017). Most results of the
SwCl analyses were statistically significant, favoring VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared to
ELX/TEZ/IVA; however, the clinical meaningfulness of the SwCl results is not established at
this time.
66
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Background
SwCl concentration measurements have been secondary endpoints in multiple clinical
development programs for CF, including ELX/TEZ/IVA. The Division views SwCl as an
important pharmacodynamic measurement for drugs that act on CFTR. However, as SwCl
measurements do not reflect how a patient feels, functions, or survives, they are not considered
(b) (4)
clinically meaningful endpoints, nor have they been validated as surrogate endpoints.
(see Section 6.2.2).
SwCl concentrations are part of the CF diagnostic algorithm (Farrell et al. 2017) but are not used
clinically as prognostic or predictive markers. There are no clinical care guidelines that
recommend serial SwCl measurements after diagnosis of CF or recommend repeating SwCl
measurements following initiation of therapy to assess response to intervention. Therefore,
information about post-treatment SwCl is of unclear meaning to prescribers and patients.
(b) (4)

the Applicant cited data
regarding the natural history of CF, drawn primarily from the U.S. Cystic Fibrosis Foundation
Patient Registry, which suggests SwCl levels at diagnosis may be prognostic for clinical
outcomes, including rate of pulmonary function decline and mortality (McKone et al. 2015).
However, other studies that have evaluated whether SwCl may predict severity of CF phenotype
have shown inconsistent results (Wilschanski et al. 1995; Davis et al. 2004). In addition, there is
no evidence to support that post-treatment SwCl concentrations will have predictive value for
clinically meaningful outcomes, such as rate of lung function decline or mortality.
We note that the clinical development program for ELX/TEZ/IVA established a large and
sustained treatment effect as measured by the ppFEV1 for the overall phase 3 population enrolled
in the registrational trials. In a postmarket study that evaluated both the change in ppFEV1 and
change in SwCl six months after initiation of ELX/TEZ/IVA, there was a wide range of
variability in the correlation between outcomes, i.e., subjects with minimal SwCl changes with a
large FEV1 response and subjects with large SwCl changes with a minimal FEV1 response
(Nichols et al. 2022). These data suggest that reliance on changes in SwCl alone will not predict
clinically meaningful outcomes.
Additional published data from an observational study comparing pre- and post-ELX/TEZ/IVA
SwCl levels also demonstrate heterogeneity in SwCl response to treatment. This study included
variations in post-ELX/TEZ/IVA SwCl levels between CFTR genotype groupings (e.g.,
F508del/MF versus F508del/F508del, in which patients had one versus two ELX/TEZ/IVA
responsive variants, respectively) as well as variation in response within a CFTR genotype
grouping (Zemanick et al. 2024). These data further limit the potential for SwCl as a predictive
marker in this context.
Assessment
Trials 102 and 103 included multiple key and other secondary endpoints related to SwCl
concentration measurements. A summary of baseline SwCl measurements in the trial population
and SwCl-based secondary endpoint results are included in Table 34.

Change From Baseline
As shown in Table 34, treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA led to a statistically significant
reduction in SwCl concentration from baseline compared to treatment with ELX/TEZ/IVA in
both phase 3 trials, with mean reductions in SwCl from baseline of -8.4mmol/L and -2.8mmol/L
in Trials 102 and 103, respectively. The clinical meaningfulness of this change is unclear. Since
SwCl is not a clinically meaningful endpoint, there is no established threshold for quantifying
what magnitude of change may be clinically significant, nor has the Applicant proposed such a
threshold. However, we note that the changes observed are numerically small. The difference in
the magnitude of change between the two trials is also of unclear significance and may be a
function of genotypic differences in SwCl at baseline, as discussed more below. The Division
maintains that these results provide pharmacodynamic information to prescribers demonstrating
(b) (4)
comparable on-targets effects of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA
Responder Analyses <60mmol/L and <30mmol/L
(b) (4)

The Applicant also proposed the use of pooled responder analyses
As shown in Table 34, subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA were more likely
to have a SwCl concentration <60mmol/L or <30mmol/L compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA at 24
weeks, based on pooled responder analyses. These threshold values are based on the CF
diagnostic threshold (60mmol/L) and normal SwCl range (<30mmol/L). As discussed above, we
do not agree that the thresholds proposed by the Applicant are clinically meaningful—these

68
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
thresholds are valid for diagnostic purposes, but their utility in measuring response to treatment
is not clear or established within the CF literature.
Furthermore, the Division does not agree that the Applicant’s decision to pool data for the
responder analyses from Trials 102 and 103 was clinically appropriate. The populations in Trials
102 and 103 had different CFTR variant-based eligibility criteria (see Section 15.1). Trial 102
enrolled subjects heterozygous for F508del and an MF variant (i.e., subjects with only one
variant predicted to be responsive to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA), whereas Trial 103 enrolled a wide range
of CFTR genotypes and included many subjects with two responsive variants (i.e., subjects
homozygous for F508del or heterozygous for F508del and another response variant). The
available data support that ELX/TEZ/IVA and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA are clinically effective in
patients with one or two responsive variants as measured by FEV1; however, since SwCl is a
pharmacodynamic measurement of CFTR function, differences in both the number and type of
CFTR variant may result in significant discrepancies and heterogeneity in SwCl levels, as
supported by the CF literature and observations from the trial results (Zemanick et al. 2024). The
baseline SwCl levels were different between the populations of the two trials with a mean
baseline SwCl of 53.0mmol/L and 42.8mmol/L in Trials 102 and 103, respectively. This
difference was, in part, attributable to baseline CFTR genotypic differences. As such, we
observed that there was a greater proportion of subjects in Trial 102 with a baseline SwCl
≥60mmol/L compared to Trial 103 (140 [35.2%] and 100 [17.5%] subjects, respectively) and a
lower proportion of subjects with a baseline SwCl <30mmol/L (36 [9.0%] and 152 [26.5%]
subjects, respectively). Thus, as expected, as shown in Table 34, the proportion of responders in
Trial 102 is greater than Trial 103, an important distinction that is lost in the pooled analysis. The
genotype differences and expected response to therapy between the enrolled populations of the
two phase 3 trials do not support pooling of the data.
We also do not agree that pooling these results was appropriate from a statistical perspective in
the context of two adequate and well controlled trials submitted to support substantial evidence
(b) (4)
of effectiveness.
There was no justification from the Applicant for pooling
this endpoint based on statistical considerations. Although the Applicant also included the
responder analyses for the trials individually as other secondary endpoints, these were conducted
outside of multiplicity control. In these exploratory analyses, results were generally consistent
with the other SwCl based secondary endpoints but remain of unclear clinical significance.
The Applicant’s statistical handling of the responder analyses, pooled and unpooled, included all
subjects in the analysis, whether the individual’s baseline SwCl was above or below the specified
threshold. Only a limited number of subjects had baseline SwCl values >60mmol/L in both trials,
as shown in Table 34. The Applicant partially addressed this issue by accounting for baseline
imbalance via the statistical analysis model; however, we note this as an additional limitation to
the interpretability of the results.
Conclusion
(b) (4)

The change from baseline SwCl results provide

pharmacodynamic information on CFTR function and will be communicated in the USPI with
(b) (4)
appropriate language to convey the uncertainty of the clinical meaning.

6.3.3. Additional Data Sources to Support Indication
Based on CFTR Variant
Issue
The Applicant submitted multiple types of data, including clinical trial results, in vitro models,
and scientific justification for extrapolation, to support the inclusion of a total of 303 CFTR
variants in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
Background
There are over 700 variants in the CFTR gene that are associated with CF disease causation,
although over 2,000 variants have been identified (CFF; JHU; and HSC 2024). The most
common CF-causing variant in the United States is F508del. Approximately 85% of individuals
with genotype information available are heterozygous for F508del and 44% are F508del
homozygous (CFF 2023). The reported prevalence of the next most reported CFTR variant in the
United States is significantly lower with no other variant reported in >5.0% of the population in
2023 (CFF 2023).
Given the rarity of many CFTR variants, relying on clinical trial data alone to demonstrate
efficacy is not feasible. In this context, in vitro data have supported the expansion of the
indication to additional CFTR variants for ELX/TEZ/IVA and other medications that contain one
or more of its components. The pathophysiology of CF is well understood, and the in vitro data
used has primarily been from an in vitro Ussing chamber assays using the FRT cell system to
model electrophysiologic changes (Durmowicz et al. 2018). Recently, in vitro HBE cells have
also been used to support the effectiveness of ELX/TEZ/IVA by demonstrating increased
chloride transport in the N1303K variant, which was not responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA when
expressed in the FRT system (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 and 217660/S003
uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]).
In addition, there remain CFTR variants that are not amenable to evaluation within these in vitro
models and are considered too rare to be easily evaluated in a clinical trial. Some of these
variants, including 16 ultra-rare noncanonical splice (NCS) variants, which are expected to result
in the presence of wild-type CFTR protein in a lower quantity than normal, were recently added
to the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA based on the extrapolation of efficacy and safety from a
clinical trial, in which three of these types of variants were enrolled (Trial VX21-445-124).
Extrapolation was supported by the knowledge that patients with these variants share a “residual
function” clinical phenotype with patients in the clinical trial and that this residual function
phenotype results from expression of wild-type CFTR protein, for which there is in vitro FRT
data demonstrating that ELX/TEZ/IVA robustly augments chloride transport (refer to the
supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 and 217660/S003 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11,
2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]).
70
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Assessment
The Applicant has proposed the inclusion of 303 CFTR variants in the indication for
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Refer to Table 2 in Section 1.2 and see Table 196 in Section 16.2.3 for
additional details on the data sources used to support the individual CFTR variants included in
the proposed indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
Clinical Data
The clinical data relies primarily on the demonstration of efficacy in subjects with at least one
copy of the F508del CFTR variant, as established by the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to
ELX/TEZ/IVA and the prior demonstration of efficacy of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
For the remaining 302 variants, the data sources are summarized as follows. There is clinical
data for an additional 23 variants from Trials 102 and 103, for which there is also nonclinical
electrophysiology support.
FRT Data
The Applicant submitted FRT data demonstrating an increase in chloride transport of ≥10% over
baseline with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment for 31 CFTR variants that were not responsive to
ELX/TEZ/IVA. See Section 14.1.2 for more details.
In addition, the Applicant submitted FRT data demonstrating a ≥10% over baseline in chloride
transport with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to support 74 variants that were recently added to the indication
for ELX/TEZ/IVA on December 11, 2024, based on the FRT data. The threshold of a ≥10% over
baseline in chloride transport established during the review of existing in vitro FRT data was also
leveraged to support a total of 154 CFTR variants previously demonstrated to be responsive to
ELX/TEZ/IVA, TEZ/IVA, or IVA. The use of this existing FRT data was discussed with the
Applicant during the development of VNZ/TEZ/IVA and the Division agreed this was a
reasonable approach.
Overall, the FRT data submitted and reference to previously available data are supportive of
inclusion of the proposed CFTR variants in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
HBE Data
In vitro data using HBE cells were used to support the inclusion of a single rare variant, N1303K,
in the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 &
217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]). In this
instance, HBE cells were used instead of FRT because the N1303K variant is not responsive to
ELX/TEZ/IVA in the FRT system (see P289v4). At the time, a regulatory threshold to indicate
effectiveness for ELX/TEZ/IVA using HBE cells had been not previously established, and the
measure of effectiveness was determined by comparing the response of ELX/TEZ/IVA in HBE
cells homozygous and heterozygous for N1303K to the response of TEZ/IVA in
F508del/F508del HBE cells.
Similarly, VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA demonstrated an increase in chloride transport in homozygous and
heterozygous N1303K HBE cells that was greater than the effect of TEZ/IVA on chloride
71
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
transport in F508del/F508del HBE cells, supporting inclusion of N1303K in the indication for
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Refer to Section 14.1.1 for more details of the clinical pharmacology
assessment.
Extrapolation of Efficacy in Noncanonical Splice Variants
The Applicant proposed that the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA be extrapolated to include 19
NCS variants. Based on the available data, these variants result in expression of wild-type CFTR
protein at the apical cell surface but at lower levels than normal.
These variants were added to the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA on December 11, 2024, and
included three NCS variants (i.e., 2789+5G>A, 3272-26A>G, 3849+10kbC>T), for which the
efficacy of ELX/TEZ/IVA was demonstrated in Trial VX21-445-124, and 16 ultra-rare NCS
variants, for which the efficacy and safety of ELX/TEZ/IVA was extrapolated. This
extrapolation was based on similarities in clinical phenotype (i.e., “residual function”) with the
variants included in Trial VX21-445-124 and in vitro data demonstrating that ELX/TEZ/IVA
augments wild-type CFTR function. Refer to Section 14.1.2 for additional details.
The data used to support the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in these NCS variants included the
demonstration of the overall clinical noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA with
supportive in vitro FRT data demonstrating that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA also augments wild-type
CFTR function. The data support the extrapolation of efficacy for these 19 CFTR variants based
on the conclusion that the expected response to and benefits of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA would translate
to patients with these rare NCS variants.
Conclusion
Overall, the data submitted by the Applicant to support the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in
patients with CFTR variants where clinical trial data is infeasible to study is sufficient to support
their inclusion in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Refer to Section 14 for additional details.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
The nonclinical program for NDA 218730 primarily focused on evaluation of the safety of the
new molecular entity VNZ. The Applicant established a scientific bridge to leverage the
nonclinical toxicity data from the IVA program (NDA 203188, Trade name: KALYDECO
[tablet]) to characterize the safety profile of D-IVA. The potential risks or safety concerns based
on nonclinical data for TEZ were previously reviewed in the TEZ/IVA (NDA 210491, Trade
name: SYMDEKO) and ELX/TEZ/IVA (NDA 212273, Trade name: TRIKAFTA [tablet])
programs. Overall, the nonclinical program supports the marketing application for NDA 218730.
The nonclinical safety of VNZ was evaluated in safety pharmacology studies in the rat and dog,
repeat dose toxicology studies in the rat and dog of up to 26 and 39 weeks duration, respectively;
72
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
a full battery of developmental and reproductive toxicology studies in the rat and rabbit; genetic
toxicology studies (Ames, in vitro micronucleus, and in vivo rat micronucleus assays); 6-month
transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study; a juvenile toxicology study in rats; 13-week
combination toxicology study in rats with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (see Section 13.1). A 2-year
carcinogenicity study in rats will be conducted as a postmarketing requirement (see Section 24).
In the general toxicology program for VNZ in rats, the major target organs of toxicity were the
stomach (erosion/ulcers) and liver (hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration, multifocal
hepatocellular necrosis) at exposures greater than 20 times (males) and 60 times (females) the
exposure at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 20 mg VNZ. In the general
toxicology program for VNZ in dogs, there were no potential safety concerns identified up to
100 times the exposure at the MRHD of VNZ. In a combination repeat dose toxicology study in
rats, there were no adverse findings when VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA were coadministered at doses
that produced exposures 4, 1, and 1 times in male animals and 9, 2, and 1 times in female
animals compared to the exposures at the MRHD, respectively. Safety pharmacology studies
conducted in rats indicated that VNZ does not adversely affect the CNS or respiratory systems.
The good laboratory practice human ether-a-go-go related gene assay indicated a potential QTc
prolongation risk, which was not observed upon follow-up in the dog repeat dose toxicology
studies. VNZ was negative for genotoxicity in the Ames assay, in vitro micronucleus assay in
TK6 cells, and in vivo micronucleus assay in male Sprague-Dawley rats. In a 6-month transgenic
mouse carcinogenicity study, there were no treatment-related tumor findings. Refer to
Section 8.3 and Section 8.4 for an overview of juvenile and developmental and reproduction
toxicity assessment with VNZ.
D-IVA is the deuterated isotopologue of IVA. A scientific bridge was established between DIVA and IVA in a 13-week repeat dose toxicology study in rats. In this study, the target organs
of toxicity for both IVA and D-IVA were the kidney (basophilic tubules, tubular dilatation, and
interstitial fibrosis), stomach (increased mucosal thickness), and thyroid (follicular cell
hypertrophy). There were no toxicologically significant differences in findings between the same
doses of D-IVA and IVA. Therefore, a bridge to the ivacaftor nonclinical program was justified
and no further toxicology studies with D-IVA alone were recommended.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
The VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC is not included in a specific drug class. The established
pharmaceutical class for D-IVA is a CFTR potentiator. The components VNZ and TEZ remain
unclassified but facilitate the cellular processing and trafficking of defective CFTR protein to the
cell surface.
We focus this section of the review on ELX/TEZ/IVA because it is the active comparator in
Trials 102 and 103 and shares components with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. ELX/TEZ/IVA also
represents the majority market share in prescriptions among this group of drugs, given its wider
variant-based indication and relative greater efficacy (Heijerman et al. 2019; CFF 2023). In
addition, the estimated cumulative worldwide patient exposure from marketing experience is also

73
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
significantly higher for ELX/TEZ/IVA at 160,240 person-years, more than double other related
medications (information provided by the Applicant).
Recently a Boxed Warning related to drug-induced liver injury and liver failure was added to the
United States Prescribing Information (USPI) of ELX/TEZ/IVA following the identification of
cases of liver failure leading to death or transplantation in patients without a history of liver
disease.5 The risk for drug-induced liver injury related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was a major focus of
this safety review.
In addition, the USPI of ELX/TEZ/IVA includes the following Warnings and Precautions6:
•

Hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis – as reported in postmarketing setting

•

Concomitant use with cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A inducers – coadministration is not
recommended

•

Concomitant use with CYP3A inhibitors – the dose of ELX/TEZ/IVA should be reduced
when used concomitantly with moderate or strong CYP3A inhibitors

•

Cataracts – cases of noncongenital lens opacities have been reported in pediatric patients
treated with IVA-containing regimens

The most common adverse drug reactions listed in the USPI include headache, upper respiratory
tract infection, abdominal pain, and diarrhea. Additional adverse reactions include rash, liver
function test elevations, increased creatine phosphokinase, and increased blood pressure. The
common adverse reactions were evaluated as part of the safety review of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, and
some were prespecified as adverse events of special interest (AESIs) (e.g., liver function testing
elevations, increased creatine phosphokinase, rash events).
In addition, there have been multiple postmarketing safety evaluations of ELX/TEZ/IVA and
other similar medications as reviewed below in Table 35.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket
Experiences
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is not currently approved in the United States or in any other foreign market.
No postmarketing experience is available. Relevant information regarding risks identified
through postmarketing experience of ELX/TEZ/IVA or medications that contain its components
is included in Section 7.2.

7.3.2. Expectations on Safety
Not applicable for this review.

7.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
Not applicable for this review.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
All clinical studies conducted as part of the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development program were
evaluated for safety. Given the short exposure periods and smaller sample sizes in the phase 1
and 2 studies, the focus of the safety review is on the phase 3 development program, Trials 102
and 103.
Both phase 3 trials included a 4-week ELX/TEZ/IVA run-in period followed by a 52-week
treatment period. Given similarities between the trial designs and populations, the safety data
from Trials 102 and 103 were pooled for analysis. The safety data were also reviewed from the
trials individually, and differences are noted where applicable. For detailed summaries of the
trial protocols, refer to Section 15.1.
75
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Subjects unable to tolerate ELX/TEZ/IVA were excluded from the trials. Although selecting the
population this way was clinically appropriate for the trial design, it adds a significant limitation
to the interpretation of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA safety data, as does the absence of a placebo arm for
comparison of baseline rates. As such, the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA safety data were evaluated in
relation to the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arms, as well as in the context of the known safety
profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA. We are unable to draw conclusions regarding the safety of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in patients who have previously been unable to tolerate ELX/TEZ/IVA, as
reviewed in Section 7.7.1.
The safety for pediatric subjects aged 6 through 11 years is extrapolated from the adolescent and
adult data based on comparable systemic exposures and because CF is the same disease process
across age groups. The pediatric safety data from the Trial 105 were also reviewed for the
presence of additional potential safety signals; however, the lack of a control arm limits the
overall interpretation of these data. As most of the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA exposure occurred in
Cohort B1 of Trial 105, this will be the primary focus of the pediatric safety data review.
All safety analyses were conducted in the safety analysis set, defined as all subjects who received
at least one dose of trial drug. Unless otherwise noted, all AEs presented are considered
treatment emergent adverse events, defined as AEs that started or worsened in severity on or
after the first dose of trial medication in the treatment period up until 28 days after the last dose
of the trial drug or completion of trial participation date, whichever occurred first. Adverse
events that occurred in the run-in period or after the treatment period are reviewed in Section 17.
No major data quality or integrity issues were identified that would preclude a safety review for
this NDA. There were no major issues identified with respect to recording, coding, and
characterizing AEs. The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms into the Medical Dictionary
for Regulatory Activities preferred terms for the events reported in the trials were reviewed and
found to be acceptable. The definition, grading scheme, and assessment of causality for all AEs
and serious adverse event (SAEs) were reviewed and found to be acceptable.

7.6.1.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and 103
There were no reported deaths attributable to adverse events during the treatment period in the
pooled phase 3 safety database. There were two deaths in Trial 103, summarized below, that
78
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
occurred after the completion of the treatment period. Both subjects were randomized to the
ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm.
•

(b) (6)

Subject
was a 39-year-old White female with CF complicated by CF-related
diabetes and bronchiectasis, asthma, and a history of reported alcohol use/abuse that was in
remission at the time of trial enrollment. The subject was on ELX/TEZ/IVA prior to trial
enrollment. The trial drug was discontinued on Day 354 for an SAE of distal intestinal
obstruction syndrome. In the weeks prior to this, the subject experienced AEs of moderate
vomiting and liver function test (LFT) elevations and underwent a magnetic resonance
cholangiopancreatography that demonstrated moderate hepatic steatosis, cholelithiasis with
gallbladder wall edema or thickening, and an intraductal papillary mucinous neoplasm of the
pancreas. The treating hepatologist’s impression was that the changes were related to
alcoholic steatosis and hepatitis. The subject then experienced pneumonia and a CF
pulmonary exacerbation. During this time, commercial ELX/TEZ/IVA was restarted but
discontinued again as the subject’s LFTs remained elevated. The subject’s clinical status
continued to deteriorate and progressed to respiratory failure and septic shock. The subject’s
family decided to withdraw care, and the subject died. The reported immediate cause of death
was acute respiratory distress syndrome from pneumonia with liver and renal failure listed as
other significant contributing factors.

The data provided do not suggest that this subject’s death was attributable to ELX/TEZ/IVA;
however, a component of drug contribution to progressive liver failure cannot be excluded.
•

(b) (6)

Subject
. 52-year-old White man with CF complicated by pancreatic
insufficiency, abdominal pain, and anterior liver nodularity. The subject had not previously
received ELX/TEZ/IVA or other similar medication. Trial drug was discontinued on Day 184
for the elevated LFTs. The subject reported ‘regular’ alcohol use at screening (12 beers per
week), which increased because of reported social events. On Day 211, the subject
experienced an SAE of hepatic cirrhosis and severe portal hypertension. The following week,
the subject was reported to have moderate portal vein thrombosis and a liver mass near the
pancreatic head on CT. Subsequent biopsy revealed severe adenocarcinoma of the pancreas.
The subject was discharged to home with palliative care and died a few weeks later.

We agree with the Applicant’s assessment that the cause of death was not likely related to trial
drug.

event; SAS, safety analysis set; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor

There was a numerically higher proportion of subjects (81, 16.5%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA
treatment arm than in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (68, 14.2%) who experienced an SAE
during the treatment period. The most common SAE reported was related to infective pulmonary
exacerbation of CF. This finding is not unexpected as pulmonary exacerbations are a common
complication of CF and is reviewed in more detail in Section 7.6.1.5.
Influenza was the next most common SAE and occurred in a slightly higher proportion of
subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (7, 1.5%) compared to those treated with
ELX/TEZ/IVA (3, 0.6%). To evaluate this further, the SAEs were also analyzed by system organ
class (SOC) (data not shown). The Infections and Infestations SOC was generally balanced
between treatment arms – VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (50, 10.2%), ELX/TEZ/IVA (46, 9.6%). There were
also no risk differences calculated for any SOC or individual preferred term (PT) for which the
95% confidence interval excluded the null. Additional review of SAEs related to FDA Medical
Query and time to SAE onset did not reveal any additional concerning safety signals.
All SAE narratives were reviewed. The general rates of SAEs in the pooled phase 3 trials were
similar to the treatment arm in Trial VX17-445-102 of the phase 3 development for program
ELX/TEZ/IVA. The rates of SAEs in Trials 102 and 103 were generally balanced between trials.
The small imbalance in SAEs between the pooled treatment arms is unlikely to be of clinical

significance. Additional review of hepatotoxicity, suicidal ideation, infective pulmonary
exacerbation of CF, and influenza is included in Section 7.6.1.5 and Section 7.6.1.6.

The AEs leading to treatment discontinuation or interruption that occurred in ≥2 subjects are

numerically higher proportion of subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm discontinued
the trial drug because of abnormalities in LFTs.

between arms. The clinical significance of the imbalance in drug interruptions is unclear.

Many of the most common AEs reported are consistent with the known safety profile of
ELX/TEZ/IVA and expected event rates in the CF population. The most common AE reported
was infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF. There was a numerically higher proportion of
subjects in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (158, 32.2%) with this AE compared to the
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (133, 27.7%), although the risk difference did not exclude the
null (risk difference [95% CI]: -4.5 [-10.2, 1.3]). Pulmonary exacerbations of CF are common in
the CF population and were also recorded as other secondary efficacy endpoints. The analysis of
the rate differences of pulmonary exacerbations of CF as an efficacy endpoint in both phase 3
trials was supportive of the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The clinical significance of the
imbalance in reported AEs related to infective pulmonary exacerbations of CF is unclear.
The noninferiority design of the phase 3 trials and high background rates of AEs related to CF
limit the ability to adjudicate adverse reactions related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. To further evaluate
the safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, AEs occurring at a higher rate in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
arm were reviewed and are outlined in Table 42.

The proportion of subjects with an AE related to influenza was higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
treatment arm (52, 10.8%) compared to those treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA (26, 5.3%). Influenza
was one of the few AEs for which the 95% confidence interval of the risk difference excluded
the null (risk difference [95% CI]: 5.5% [2.2, 9.1]). As noted in Section 7.6.1.3, the rate of SAEs
related to influenza was also numerically higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm. The
significance of this difference is unclear. Additional PTs of upper respiratory tract infections and
specific viral upper respiratory tract infections were also higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm;
however, notably, coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) occurred at a higher rate in the
ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (127, 25.9%) compared to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (107, 22.3%). Overall, the viral
infections observed did not contribute to increased rates of infective exacerbations of CF or
hospitalizations. We also note that rates of AEs related to influenza were also elevated compared
to placebo in the clinical development program for ELX/TEZ/IVA, but the clinical relevance of
this finding was also considered uncertain at the time.
Adverse events related to rash, transaminase elevations, and increased creatinine phosphokinase
are discussed in Section 7.6.1.6. Other AEs reported, including cough, sinus congestion,
abdominal pain, and nasal congestion, are often symptoms associated with CF. The interpretation
of these, and other reported AEs such as headache and pyrexia, is limited due to the lack of a
placebo arm to assess the baseline rate of these events in the trial population.
We note the following AEs occurred at higher rates in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm with at
least a 1% difference in the risk difference: infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF, COVID-19,

sinusitis, vomiting, arthralgia, dyspnea, constipation, and back pain. The clinical implications of
these differences between the treatment arms are also unclear.
Review of the phase 3 trials individually and by SOC did not reveal any additional concerning
safety signals. Because of the limitations from the trial design and high rate of background AEs
related to CF, we recommend the inclusion of all AEs occurring in ≥5% of treated subjects
occurring at a greater rate in VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treated subjects compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA be
included in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
Overall, the review of common AEs did not demonstrate significant new safety concerns for
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA beyond the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA. The analysis of the data
suggests that the safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is very similar to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
7.6.1.6. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Trials 102 and
103
The following adverse events were prespecified as AESIs based on the known safety profile of
medications similar to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (e.g., ELX/TEZ/IVA) and safety information from the
phase 2 development program: liver function test abnormalities, creatine kinase elevations, rash,
cataracts, hypoglycemic events, and neuropsychiatric events.
Liver Function Test Abnormalities and Related Events
Transaminase elevations are a well described adverse reaction related to all approved
medications that affect CFTR processing or function and have been included as a Warning and
Precaution in the USPI of these products since the initial approval of IVA in 2012
(Vertex Pharmaceuticals 2012). During the course of the NDA review of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, an
FDA Amendments Act of 2007 safety labeling change dated October 22, 2024, was issued for
the addition of a Boxed Warning to the ELX/TEZ/IVA USPI regarding the risk of drug-induced
liver injury and liver failure attributable to ELX/TEZ/IVA. This change was based on data from
clinical trials submitted to a recent supplemental NDA (refer to the supplemental NDAs
212273/S012 & 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID:
5493844]) and review of the available postmarketing data.
Given the similarities between VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA, the DILI team in the
Division of Hepatology and Nutrition (DHN) was consulted to assist in the review of the
potential DILI risk related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. An Information Request was sent to the
Applicant on July 16, 2024, to obtain additional information on cases of suspected DILI that
were identified in the initial safety review. The DILI team within DHN also assisted the primary
review team in the assessment of the individual cases and the risk for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA-related
DILI was discussed at multiple internal meetings within the Division and Office of New Drugs.
There were no cases of liver failure leading to death or liver transplant noted in the phase 3
pooled safety database for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. As noted, the review of the safety database is
limited both by the lack of a placebo arm and the required exclusion of subjects intolerant of
ELX/TEZ/IVA. There were five cases of nonjaundiced DILI that were assessed as ‘probably’ or
‘possibly’ related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, reviewed in more detail below. No subjects exposed to
84
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had transaminase elevations with concurrent elevations in total bilirubin. One
ELX/TEZ/IVA exposed subject did meet Hy’s Law. Summaries of the cases assessed as
probably related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA are reviewed below:
•

(b) (6)

Subject
was a 48-year-old White man with CF and Gilbert’s syndrome. The
subject had been randomized to the ELX/TEZ/D-IVA arm during the treatment period of
Trial 102 and continued to the open-label extension (Trial 104) and began treatment with
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. On Day 256 of Trial 104, the subject’s transaminases were >10x the
upper limit of normal (ULN) alanine aminotransferase [ALT] 813 U/L [14 x ULN] and
aspartate aminotransferase [AST] 395 U/L [11 x ULN]) and the total bilirubin was
2.53 mg/dL (>2 x ULN). The bilirubin elevation noted was of unclear clinical significance
because of the subject’s history of Gilbert’s syndrome. The trial drug was discontinued the
following day, and the subject withdrew from the trial on Day 307. Targeted laboratory
evaluation that included hepatitis antibodies and antigens and evaluation for cytomegalovirus
(CMV) and Epstein-Barr virus was unremarkable. An ultrasound revealed a liver of normal
size and morphology. We note that the subject was also reported to have an AE related to a
respiratory tract infection (viral respiratory tract infection by metapneumovirus) on Day 268.

We agree with the Applicant’s assessment that given the context of an unrevealing work-up and
lack of alternative etiology, the elevation in the transaminases is related to hepatocellular injury
due to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. We are unable to assess the clinically meaningfulness of the elevation
in the total bilirubin because of the subject’s history of Gilbert’s disease. There is no evidence to
support this event was related to the metapneumovirus respiratory tract infection.
•

(b) (6)

Subject
was a 40-year-old White man with CF complicated by pancreatic
insufficiency who was receiving treatment with IVA monotherapy prior to enrollment. The
subject was randomized to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm. On Day 116, the subject had
AEs reported for increased ALT and AST. The initial elevations in the transaminases were
>5 x ULN (ALT 371 U/L and AST 189 U/L) and the data available in the case narrative
demonstrated that the transaminase levels peaked at on Day 120 (ALT 615 U/L [>10 x ULN]
and AST 318 U/L [>8 x ULN]). Total bilirubin levels were within the normal range.
Targeted laboratory evaluation that included hepatitis antibodies and antigens, evaluation for
CMV and Epstein-Barr virus, and an abdominal ultrasound were unremarkable. The subject
received the last dose of trial drug on Day 120 and withdrew consent and discontinued from
the trial a few days later.
The Applicant assessed that that the role of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in this case cannot be
excluded. Addition information was requested from the Applicant on September 16, 2024, to
further assess this case but the data available was limited as the follow-up clinical evaluation
was done outside of the clinical trial setting. The provided data revealed that the
transaminases continued to rise, with a highest ALT of 1130 U/L (>28 x ULN) and a highest
AST of 436 U/L (>9 x ULN) and confirmed that the total bilirubin remained within the
normal range; however, the data did not reveal a peak bilirubin. No additional follow up
information was available. Our assessment of this case is that it is probably hepatocellular
injury related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.

•

(b) (6)

Subject
was a 40-year-old White man enrolled in Trial VX23-121-012, a phase
1, randomized, open-label relative bioavailability, food effect, and dose proportionality of a
granule formulation of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in healthy adult subjects. The subject had a
reported past medical history that included hepatitis A, parasitic infection (no other
information available), and ‘positive’ CMV (no other information available). All LFT
parameters were reported to be in the normal range at the screening visit prior to the first
dose of the trial drug. On Day 20, six days after the second dose of the trial drug, the subject
had AEs of increased ALT, AST, and gamma-glutamyl transferase reported. The ALT was
537 U/L (>8 x ULN), the AST was 219 U/L (>4 x ULN), and the gamma-glutamyl
transferase was 269 U/L (>3 x ULN). The total bilirubin remained within the normal range.
Subsequent follow up laboratory analysis revealed down trending transaminases. Targeted
laboratory evaluation that included hepatitis antibodies and antigens and evaluation for CMV
and Epstein-Barr virus was unremarkable; however, the available data are limited because no
dates were provided by the Applicant. An abdominal ultrasound was notable for mild
steatosis.
The Applicant, in conjunction with their hepatology consultant, assessed that the subject
likely had underlying nonalcohol steatohepatitis with an acute asymptomatic transaminase
elevation that resolved after trial drug discontinuation and that an association with
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA cannot be excluded. Our assessment of this case was that, regardless of
potential undiagnosed nonalcohol steatohepatitis, the acute transaminase event was probably
hepatocellular injury related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.

The overall assessment of the reviewed cases was that, although there were no cases of
concurrent transaminitis and bilirubin elevation, the available data supports that there is a risk of
hepatocellular injury associated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
In addition, the maximum liver function test elevations from baseline occurring during the
treatment period were evaluated in the pooled phase 3 safety database and are outlined below in
Table 43. The available data to assess for maximum LFT elevations from baseline was limited by
the exclusion of subjects with a history of intolerance to ELX/TEZ/IVA. Note that >85% of the
enrolled phase 3 population had documented prior use of ELX/TEZ/IVA or another medication
containing its components. These limitations may lead to a rate of LFT elevations in the
ELX/TEZ/IVA that is lower than that seen in the ELX/TEZ/IVA registrational trials or observed
in clinical care, but we are unable to assess the impact of these limitation on the subjects exposed
to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.

Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elexacaftor;

There was a higher proportion of subjects with transaminase elevations in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
treatment arm compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA. When evaluating maximum elevations in either ALT
or AST, 29 (6.0%) subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm had transaminase elevations
>3 x ULN compared to 15 (3.1%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm. Similarly, six (1.3%)
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treated subjects had either ALT or AST elevations >8 x ULN compared to one
(0.2%) subject treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA. However, when evaluating for elevations in total
bilirubin, a higher proportion of subjects (21, 4.3%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm had
elevations in total bilirubin between 2 to 3 x ULN compared to subjects (8, 1.7%) treated with
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. No subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had total bilirubin elevations >3 x
ULN, compared to 8 (1.6%) subjects treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA.
The clinical implications of the differences between the rates of transaminase and bilirubin
elevations between the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arms are unclear, but the
overall assessment of the available data is that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA likely conveys a similar level
of risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA. An additional analysis of the shift in LFTs from baseline
was unrevealing (data not shown).

Similar to the analysis of the maximum postbaseline laboratory elevations, subjects treated with
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA were more likely to have transaminase-related AEs recorded (43, 9.0%)
compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA treated subjects (35, 7.1%). In general, the transaminase related AEs
were also more likely to be severe in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treated subjects: four (0.8%) versus
two (0.4%) in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm and ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arms,
respectively. However, overall SAEs were the same between treatment arms: two (0.4%)
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA exposed subjects versus two (0.4%) ELX/TEZ/IVA exposed subjects. There
were also more subjects (7, 1.5%) in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm with transaminase
related AEs leading to treatment discontinuation than those subjects (3, 0.6%) treated with
ELX/TEZ/IVA.
The ability to interpret the clinical meaningfulness of the noted differences between treatment
arms is limited by the absence of a placebo and the population limited to those tolerant of
ELX/TEZ/IVA; however, the data support the conclusion that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys at least
a similar risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA. Additional assessment of the potential for
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA induced liver injury including labeling recommendations is included in
Section 7.7.2.
Creatine Kinase Elevations
Section 6 of the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA includes the adverse reaction of ‘increased creatine
phosphokinase’ noting that the incidence of maximum creatine phosphokinase was greater in
ELX/TEZ/IVA-treated subjects compared to placebo and that some cases were associated with
treatment interruption or rhabdomyolysis. Creatine phosphokinase elevation was designated as
an AESI in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development program. Table 45 reviews the in-depth
assessment of creatine phosphokinase elevations in the pooled phase 3 safety database.

Overall, there were similar rates of creatine phosphokinase AEs between treatment arms;
however, there was a small numerical increase in the overall number of increased blood
creatinine phosphokinase AEs reported (VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA 43 [9.0%] subjects compared to

treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA (32, 6.5%). The majority of AEs were mild to moderate. There were
two SAEs, one in each treatment arm, leading to trial drug discontinuation and adjudicated as
possibly related to trial drug. Both subjects had previously experienced elevations of creatine
phosphokinase while on ELX/TEZ/IVA that were attributed to exercise. There were no cases of
rhabdomyolysis reported.
It is difficult to assess the clinical implication of the rates of creatine phosphokinase elevations
without a placebo, but our review suggests that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys at least a similar risk
to ELX/TEZ/IVA for creatinine phosphokinase elevations. We recommend continued inclusion
of this risk in Section 6 of the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
Cataract Adverse Events
Cataracts have been observed in subjects, particularly pediatric subjects, treated with IVA
monotherapy and in juvenile rat toxicology studies with IVA. The risk for cataract is currently
included in Section 5 of the USPI for all IVA containing medications including ELX/TEZ/IVA.
Trials 102 and 103 required ophthalmologic exams at screening/baseline and at Week 24 for
pediatric subjects <18 years of age. Adverse events related to cataracts, including those occurring
in subjects ≥18 years, are reviewed in Table 46.

determined to be negligible and no further action was necessary beyond routine
pharmacovigilance. The two cases of AEs related to congenital cataracts were recorded in
adolescent subjects receiving the trial drug themselves and are not related to this congenital
cataract NISS.

Overall, the majority of rash-related AEs reported were mild and there were no SAEs or lifethreatening AEs related to rashes in either treatment arm. Only one severe event was reported in
a subject who was a 28-year-old White female with CF and a history of contact dermatitis and
multiple drug hypersensitivity events, previously taking ELX/TEZ/IVA. The subject was
randomized to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm and reported a rash described as
maculopapular on Day 8. The following day, the rash had spread to include the feet, legs, chest,
back, arm, and face. Symptoms progressed to include dysphonia and the subject described a
raspy voice, ‘itchy’ sensation in her throat, and lip swelling. She was then hospitalized for the
SAE of hypersensitivity. This AEs was likely related to the trial drug but appears consistent with
the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
There is a numerical increase in rash and other skin-related AEs in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
treatment arm compared the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (risk difference [95% CI]: 3.3% [-0.4,7.1]). It is
difficult to assess this safety signal in the absence of a placebo arm and in a population generally
tolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA. The imbalance in rash events between the treatment arms was most
pronounced in the first 4-6 weeks of therapy. At Day 40 there were 29 subjects (6.0%) with rash
events reported in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm compared to 10 subjects (2.0%) in the
ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (data not shown).
The clinical significance of the increased number of events or timing of events in the
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm is not clear. However, the data suggest that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
conveys at least a similar risk to ELX/TEZ/IVA for rashes and support inclusion of relevant data
in Section 6 of the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA USPI.

The overall rates of AEs related to hypoglycemia events were low; however, they were
numerically higher in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (18, 3.7%) compared to the
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (8, 1.7%). The majority of AEs were mild to moderate, with

In the targeted review of the blood glucose laboratory analyses (data not shown), the rates of
hypoglycemia <70 mg/dL were higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm compared to the
ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (168, 35.0% versus 151, 30.8% respectively); however, the rates of more
severe hypoglycemia (<54 and <40 mg/dL) were rare and balanced between treatment arms.
The rates of fasting hyperglycemia ranging from 100 to 125 mg/dL were also elevated in the
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm (91, 32.4%) compared to the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (72, 23.9%), although
this was in contrast to the rates of random (i.e., not fasting) hyperglycemia (≥200 mg/dL), that
were more elevated in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (70, 16.5%) compared to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
arm (70, 16.5%).
The clinical implication of the imbalances of serum glucose levels is unclear, particularly with a
small overall incidence of related AEs. Overall, these data do not suggest an overt safety concern
at this time.
Neuropsychiatric Events
Baseline mental health disorders, including depression and anxiety, are elevated in patients with
CF compared to their aged-matched peers. Some published data suggest that medications that
affect CFTR processing or function can have a beneficial impact on the mental health of CF
patients on a population level (Piehler et al. 2024). However, there have been postmarketing
safety reports suggesting that there may be increased rates of depression, including suicidality, in
some CF patients taking these medications. Psychiatric events are also currently being evaluated
as a postmarketing safety signal (SSID #1005257), and neuropsychiatric events were included as
an AESI for the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development program to further evaluate this safety concern.
The in-depth assessment of neuropsychiatric AEs is included in Table 49.

neuropsychiatric events reported.

suicidality in both treatment arms. Most cases had confounding factors, which, in addition to the
lack of a placebo arm, make it difficult to assess causality. These data do not suggest that

ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
ELX/TEZ/IVA. Overall, the relationship between these medications and neuropsychiatric events
remains incompletely described. This safety signal will be further evaluated in the ongoing NISS
(SSID 1005257). Currently, the data do no support inclusion in the USPI.
7.6.1.7. Review of Postmarketing Safety Signals in Similar
Drug Products
The phase 3 safety data were also reviewed for signals from ongoing post marketing safety
reviews or previous reviews for which labeling changes were issued (outlined in Section 7.2).
Note that two open NISS review topics, hepatoxicity and neuropsychiatric events, were
designated as AESIs and are reviewed in more detail in the above Section 7.6.1.6.
Hypersensitivity Reactions (SSID 1004722)
A Warning and Precaution related to hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis, was
added to the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA and other medications that contain one or more of its
components as result of a safety labeling change issued on August 3, 2023. An evaluation of AEs
based on PTs, SOC, FDA medical queries (broad and narrow) was performed. There were no
overt cases of anaphylaxis or angioedema reported; however, there were multiple AEs related to
hypersensitivity events in both treatment arms, including the case described in Section 7.6.1.6.
We recommend that the Warning and Precaution related to hypersensitivity reactions be included
in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA based on these hypersensitivity AEs noted, the shared or
similar APIs in both FDCs, and the overall similarity in safety profiles between VNZ/TEZ/DIVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA.
Increased Intracranial Pressure Disorders (SSID 1005337)
A NISS was opened to assess for the presence of increased intracranial pressure disorders on
May 16, 2024, and remained under evaluation at the time of this review. To assess this risk in the
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA phase 3 safety database, a review of potential PTs related to increased
intracranial pressure disorders included in the nervous system disorders, vascular disorders, and
eye disorder SOCs was conducted (data not shown). There did not appear to be any PTs directly
related to increased intracranial pressure occurring in either treatment arm in the phase 3
development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. This signal will continue to be monitored via
routine postmarket pharmacovigilance unless otherwise indicated by results of the ongoing
NISS.
7.6.1.8. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and
103
Analyses of clinical laboratory results including chemistry, hematologic parameters, and total
cholesterol, consisting of changes in mean values over time, changes in individual subjects over
time, and potentially clinically significant values were conducted. There were no clinically
meaningful trends or mean changes from baseline observed. Postbaseline newly occurring or
worsening potentially clinically significant values for hematology and clinical chemistry were
infrequent and similar between the treatment arms.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
7.6.1.9. Vital Signs, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and 103
No clinically significant changes in mean vital sign values (systolic and diastolic blood pressure,
heart rate, and respiratory rate) over time were identified in the pooled phase 3 safety database.
We note an increase rate of diastolic blood pressure readings below 60 mmHg in the
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (141, 29.4%) compared to the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm
(111, 22.6%); however, there was a similar proportion of subjects with systolic blood pressure
readings <90 mmHg. These readings do not appear clinically significant as there were very low
rates of hypotension related AEs reported during the treatment period.
Increased blood pressure is included in Section 6 of the current USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA. Review
of maximum blood pressure readings and AEs related to hypertension did not reveal a significant
difference between the treatment arms; however, 90 (18.8%) of subjects treated with
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had a systolic blood pressure reading over 140 mm Hg compared to 90
(18.3%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm. We recommend that increased blood pressure be included in
Section 6 of the USPI of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
Overall, no significant new safety concerns were identified in the analysis of vital signs.

differences noted between subgroups.

Cataract Adverse Events

Rash-Related Adverse Events
There were four (5.1%) subjects that experienced a rash event during the treatment period of
Cohort B1 of Trial 105. All were assessed as mild in severity.
Hypoglycemic Events
There were no AEs reported for hypoglycemia in Cohort B1 of Trial 105. On review of the
clinical laboratory results, 23 (29.5%) subjects had a blood glucose result of <70 mg/dL,
although only 1 (1.3%) subject had results <54 mg/dL. The clinical significance of this is unclear
without a comparator treatment arm.
Neuropsychiatric Events
There were four (5.1%) subjects that experienced neuropsychiatric AEs that included aggression,
anxiety, and initial insomnia. All were assessed as mild, with none resulting in treatment
discontinuation or interruption.
Review of the AESIs that occurred in Cohort B1 of Trial 105 did not reveal any new safety
concerns.
7.6.2.7. Laboratory Findings, Trial 105
Analysis of clinical laboratory results including chemistry, hematologic parameters, and total
cholesterol, consisting of changes in mean values over time, changes in individual subjects over
time, and potentially significant values were conducted. There were no clinically meaningful
trends or mean changes from baseline were observed. The interpretation of these data is limited
by the lack of a comparator, but, overall, the assessment of laboratory findings was reassuring
and did not reveal any new safety signals.
7.6.2.8. Vital-Sign Analyses, Trial 105
There were no clinically significant changes in mean vital sign values (systolic and diastolic
blood pressure, heart rate, respiratory rate, and temperature) over time that occurred during the
treatment period for Cohort B1 of Trial 105. Although limited by the lack of a comparator arm,
no significant safety concerns were identified.
7.6.2.9. Subgroup Analyses, Trial 105
A safety analysis by key demographic subgroup, including gender at birth and region (U.S. and
ex-U.S. sites) did not reveal any clinically significant differences between subgroups.

An additional subgroup analysis by subject’s weight-based dosing cohort at baseline (<40 mg

n=8), but did not reveal a clinically significant difference in the rates of AEs.
7.6.2.10. Pediatric (6 Through 11 Years of Age) Safety
Summary
The safety results from Trial 105 did not reveal new safety signals or identify imbalances in AEs
to suggest differential risks for children compared to adolescents or adults. However, given the
single arm design of the trial and the small sample size, interpretation of these results is limited.
Safety for the 6-to-11-year-old CF population was, therefore, extrapolated from the adolescent
and adult populations enrolled in the phase 3 development program. Treatment with
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA leads to comparable systemic exposures between populations, and there are
sufficient similarities in disease process across age groups to support extrapolation. Such an
approach is consistent with the development of related products (e.g., ELX/TEZ/IVA) for
pediatric CF patients.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Assessment of Safety in Subjects Intolerant of
ELX/TEZ/IVA
Issue
Alternative therapies for CF patients who are unable to tolerate ELX/TEZ/IVA or a medication
that contains its components is an area of unmet need for CF therapeutic development. However,
to maintain clinical equipoise, the phase 3 development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA required
a noninferiority trial design that necessitated the exclusion of subjects intolerant of
ELX/TEZ/IVA. The available data do not assess safety and tolerability in the ELX/TEZ/IVAintolerant population.
Background
ELX/TEZ/IVA and other medications that affect CFTR processing or function have contributed
to many clinical benefits in eligible CF patients, and over 80% of the U.S. CF population are
prescribed one of these treatments. However, there remains an unmet medical need for
alternative CF therapies for patients who are not currently eligible based on CFTR genotype as
well as for patients who are intolerant of, or with persistent symptoms despite treatment with, the
currently approved medications. The CF Foundation estimates that approximately 3,000 patients,
almost 10% of the U.S. CF population, are currently genotype eligible but not prescribed one of
these therapies (CFF 2023). Although many reasons contribute to this fact, intolerance to therapy
is a primary reason for discontinuation of treatment in these patients.
The Applicant chose a noninferiority trial design with ELX/TEZ/IVA as the active control for
the phase 3 development program of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Given the magnitude of treatment effect
and pervasive use of ELX/TEZ/IVA as the standard of care, the Division agreed to this design
101
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
(reviewed in more detail in Section 6.2.2.1). The noninferiority design necessitated that subjects
with a history of intolerance to ELX/TEZ/IVA be excluded. The disadvantages of this approach
are the inability to characterize the safety and tolerability in the ELX/TEZ/IVA-intolerant
population and the limited generalizability of the observed results.
Assessment
In general, the safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA appear comparable. As reviewed
in Section 7.6.1, there were similar rates of overall AEs, severe AEs, and SAEs. The most
common AEs were also similar between the treatment arms, as were the overall rates of AESIs.
There were no new safety signals identified. These findings are reassuring since we anticipate
that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA has the potential to replace ELX/TEZ/IVA as the standard of care.
However, these similarities in safety profile also suggest that patients intolerant of
ELX/TEZ/IVA may experience similar adverse reactions to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
To further characterize the safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, we performed additional
subgroup analyses on subjects that had not previously been prescribed ELX/TEZ/IVA or a
medication containing its components prior to enrollment. We note that this analysis aims to
characterize the safety profile in treatment naïve subjects but will not characterize the safety
profile in ELX/TEZ/IVA intolerant patients.
The analysis of the subgroup of subjects not previously prescribed ELX/TEZ/IVA or its
components is very limited by sample size, as this subgroup only accounted for 15% of total
subjects. In addition, all subjects received ELX/TEZ/IVA for four weeks during the run-in period
prior to randomization and were, therefore, not entirely treatment naïve upon starting the
treatment period.
Subjects in this subgroup had a baseline mean ppFEV1 of 71.0% and a mean baseline SwCl of
52.2mmol/L. They were more likely to have a non-F508del TCR variant (20% vs 1.8%) and less
likely to have F/F or F/MF variants compared to non-naïve subjects. The safety results in this
group are summarized in Table 56.

In the run-in period, 74 (51%) of subjects in this subgroup experienced an AE over the 4 weeks
with two (1.4%) subjects experiencing SAEs, one infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF and
one rash. This rate is higher than the rate of AEs in run-in period in the overall population (395,
38.3%) reviewed in Section 17.2. The clinical significance of this is unclear given the limitations
of the sample size and duration under observation. The most common AEs reported during the
run-in period in treatment naïve subjects were consistent with the known safety profile of
ELX/TEZ/IVA and similar to the AEs reported during the treatment period, which included
headache, cough, and rash. Infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF was only reported in one
(0.7%) subject, which is likely attributable to the short treatment duration of the run-in period.
AEs leading to discontinuation in the run-in occurred in five (3.4%) subjects and included
COVID-19, influenza, and rash. One (0.7%) additional subject had trial treatment interrupted due
to rash.
In the treatment period, the overall rates of AEs were similar between arms–VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
60 (84.5%) subjects and ELX/TEZ/IVA 55 (85.9%) subjects. We note the proportion of subjects
with AEs is lower than the overall population – 459 (95.6%) versus 469 (95.5%) subjects in
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA, respectively (see Section 7.6.1.5). The clinical
significance of this difference is unclear but may be related to underlying differences in baseline
CF disease.
There were more subjects experiencing AEs leading to discontinuation in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm
(4, 6.2%) compared to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm (2, 2.8%). The AEs that led to discontinuation
were all related to LFT abnormalities except for one (1.6%) subject in the ELX/TEZ/IVA
treatment arm. The clinical implication of this is unclear, since in the overall population, AEs
related to LFT abnormalities and leading to treatment discontinuation were higher in the
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm. In addition, the rates of LFT elevations in the subgroup of treatment
103
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
naïve subjects were higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm compared to the
ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (data not shown).
Overall, our assessment of the subgroup of subjects not previously prescribed CFTR-directed
therapies supports the conclusion that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys a similar safety risk profile to
ELX/TEZ/IVA.
Conclusion
The available data support that the risks of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA are
comparable; however, there are no data to characterize the safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in patients
intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA or medications that contain its components. The Division
recommends labeling strategies to address this uncertainty. We do not recommend a limitation of
use since the potential benefit of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in this population is substantial. We expect
that additional characterization of the safety comparability of these products may only be
clarified in the postmarket setting. To guide patients and providers on the risks of VNZ/TEZ/DIVA in the ELX/TEZ/IVA-intolerant population, we will include a Warning and Precaution that
clearly describes the absence of data in patients intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA and guides
prescribers to weigh benefits and risks before initiating VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in this population. In
addition, as discussed throughout this review, the serious and well-characterized risks of
ELX/TEZ/IVA will be retained in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.

7.7.2. Risk for Hepatotoxicity With VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
Issue
Transaminase elevations are a well described adverse reaction related to all approved
medications that affect CFTR processing or function and have been included as a Warning and
Precaution in the USPI since the initial approval of IVA in 2012 (Vertex Pharmaceuticals 2012).
Liver toxicity findings were also noted in the VNZ toxicology program (Section 7.1) and
previously documented in the IVA toxicology program (NDA 203188). The risk for potential
hepatoxicity related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was a major focus of the safety review.
Background
The hepatoxicity risks listed on the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA had previously included elevated
liver transaminases with and without elevated bilirubin as well as liver failure leading to
transplantation in one subject with underlying cirrhosis and portal hypertension. As discussed in
Section 7.6.1.6, this warning was elevated to a Boxed Warning for DILI and liver failure as part
of a recent safety labeling change, dated October 22, 2024. In addition, a NISS (SSID 1005364)
was opened to include queries for liver injury data across all approved products containing at
least one component of ELX/TEZ/IVA. This NISS is in the action phase pending finalization at
the time of this review.

104
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Assessment
To assess the hepatoxicity risk of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, LFT abnormalities were pre-specified as an
AESI in the phase 3 development program, and results are reviewed in detail in Section 7.6.1.6.
The rates of transaminase laboratory elevations and AEs related to transaminases were higher in
the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm than in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (Table 57).
