Although it is difficult to interpret this difference given that over 85% of the population had been
treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA or its components prior to enrollment, these data suggest that
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys a similar risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
We further assessed the rates of AST and ALT elevations in subjects that were not previously
prescribed ELX/TEZ/IVA or its components (the subgroup described in Section 7.7.1).
Elevations in AST or ALT 3 to 5 x ULN were noted in four (5.8%) subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/DIVA treatment arm compared to one (1.6%) subject in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm. In
addition, one (1.5%) subject in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had an AST or ALT elevation greater than
8x the ULN compared to no subjects treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA. Overall, these data further
suggest that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys at least a similar risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
On review of the individual cases, there were five cases of nonjaundiced DILI assessed as
probably or possibly related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, including a case in a healthy volunteer. Refer
to DHN consult dated December 19, 2024, for details (DARRTS ID: 5499379). There were no
cases of Hy’s law or documented cases of liver failure attributed to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. However,
the available phase 3 safety database of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is limited to 480 subjects treated for
52 weeks. Although this scope is sufficient to determine the overall safety profile of
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA for chronic use, it is unlikely to capture rare events, particularly when
compared to the estimated cumulative exposure of ELX/TEZ/IVA of 160,240 person-years (see
Section 7.2).
Finally, we note that the etiology of the liver injury related to ELX/TEZ/IVA has not been
established. In the ELX/TEZ/IVA clinical program, the trough concentrations of ELX, TEZ,
IVA, and their active metabolites reported from six subjects who experienced hepatotoxicity
were all within the therapeutic range (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 &
217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]). The
mechanism of injury is likely an off-target effect, mediated by one or more components of
ELX/TEZ/IVA. That VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA shares the same (TEZ) and two highly similar
105
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
components (VNZ and ELX, IVA and D-IVA) components also supports that the risk for liver
injury is comparable to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
Given the recent addition of the Boxed Warning for DILI and liver failure to the USPI for
ELX/TEZ/IVA, there were multidisciplinary discussions held between the Division and DHN
and within the Office of New Drugs regarding the assessment of risk related to VNZ/TEZ/DIVA and how to convey this risk to prescribers. The Division will include a Boxed Warning for
potential DILI and liver failure related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. This risk is appropriate for a Boxed
Warning since it is not only serious and life-threatening, but also potentially preventable through
risk mitigation strategies. This approach is consistent with the FDA guidance for industry
Warning and Precautions, Contraindications, and Boxed Warning Sections of Labeling for
Human Prescription Drug and Biological Products — Content and Format (October 2011) for
serious risks for which the frequency or severity could be reduced by interventions (e.g.,
increased monitoring frequency, drug interruption guidelines). The guidance also reviews that
there are instances when a Boxed Warning based on an anticipated reaction would be
appropriate, as in this case.
Conclusion
There are no data to suggest that the risk of DILI with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is different from or
lower than the risk with ELX/TEZ/IVA. The Division will include a Boxed Warning, consistent
with that in the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA, in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The Boxed Warning
will include recommendations for increased LFT monitoring frequency and duration, guidelines
for drug interruption, and considerations for population selection. Additional information
regarding labeling is included in Section 23. An enhanced pharmacovigilance strategy will attach
to the approval of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to further evaluate this safety signal in the postmarket
setting.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Hepatic Impairment
The Applicant proposed no dose adjustment of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet for CF patients
(b) (4)
with mild hepatic impairment (HI) (Child-Pugh Class A)
. However, the use of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in CF patients with MHI is not
recommended and should only be considered when there is a clear medical need, and the benefit
exceeds the risk. VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA should not be used in CF patients with severe HI (ChildPugh Class C). The review team agree that this approach is acceptable, and these
recommendations will be included in the USPI.
The Applicant conducted a reduced design dedicated HI trial (Study VX21-121-008) to evaluate
a single dose of VNZ 10 mg/TEZ 50 mg /D-IVA 125 mg FDC PK in subjects with MHI (n=12)
compared to matched healthy subjects (n=12). Results of Study 008 showed that the impact of
MHI on TEZ exposure was insignificant, while the mean exposures of VNZ and D-IVA were
106
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
lower in subjects with MHI compared to those in matched healthy subjects. The total exposure
area under the concentration-time estimated to infinity (AUCinf) of VNZ and D-IVA decreased
30%and 20%, respectively, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment when compared to
matched subjects with normal hepatic function. Similarly, the free drug exposure (AUCunbound, 0inf) of VNZ and D-IVA decreased 20% and 40%, respectively, in subjects with MHI when
compared to matched subjects with normal hepatic function.
Based on the hepatotoxicity of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (refer to Section 7.7.2 and the PK results from
the dedicated HI Study VX21-121-008, the clinical pharmacology review team agrees that no
dose modification is recommended for CF patients with mild HI; VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA should not
be used in CF patients with severe HI, and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA should only be considered in
patients with MHI (without dose adjustment) when there is a clear medical need and the benefit
exceeds the risk. Because of hepatotoxicity, the clinical pharmacology review team also agrees
liver function tests should be closely monitored in HI patients on VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment.
Renal Impairment
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet has not been studied in CF patients with any degree of renal
impairment (RI) or end-stage renal disease. There were CF subjects with mild RI (n=126) and
moderate RI (n=2) in the phase 3 trials. The Applicant proposed no dose adjustment for subjects
with mild or moderate renal impairment. The USPI will recommend use with caution in people
with CF with severe RI or end-stage renal disease. The review team agrees that this approach is
acceptable.
Renal excretion contributed minimally to VNZ elimination (0.5% of the total radioactivity was
detected in the urine within 17 days postdose, as indicated by Study 004). Per TEZ/IVA labeling,
the contribution of renal route in TEZ excretion is approximately 14% (mostly as M2 metabolite)
and <1% of the administered dose was excreted in urine as unchanged TEZ. This result suggests
that renal excretion is not the major pathway of TEZ elimination in humans. Although a mass
balance study was not conducted for D-IVA, the urine excretion of D-IVA is expected to be low
based on in vitro comparison results with IVA (6.6% of IVA dose is excreted in urine). In
addition, population PK showed similar exposure of VNZ/TEZ/F-IVA between healthy subjects
and subjects with mild/moderate RI.
Overall, these results suggest that renal clearance is likely to play a minimal role in the
elimination of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA and support the proposed dosing recommendation.
Pediatrics PK
The proposed pediatric doses in children 6 through 11 years of age (i.e., VNZ 20 mg/TEZ
100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg QD for children weighing ≥40 kg and VNZ 12 mg/TEZ 60 mg/D-IVA
150 mg QD for children weighing ≥40 kg) were studied in Cohort B1 of Trial VX21-121-105
with 78 children.
The comparison of pediatric observed Ctrough values and population PK-estimated steady state
AUC values of VNZ, TEZ, M1-TEZ, D-IVA, and M1-D-IVA following the studied/proposed
doses were generally within the range of those in adults and adolescents. For details, refer to
Section 6.1.3.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Food Effect
Study VX21-121-005 (Study 005)
The effect of food consumption on drug exposure was evaluated in Part B of Study 005. Study
005 Part B was a phase 1, randomized, open-label, crossover study with 3-period, 3-sequence
design to evaluate the effect of food on the relative bioavailability of VNZ [Form D]/TEZ/DIVA FDC tablet.
The PK analysis dataset included data from 17 healthy subjects who received VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
FDC tablets in the three dosing periods: FDC Fasted, FDC Low-fat (approximately 400 to 500
kCal including 11 to 14 g of fat [approximately 25% of caloric content]), and FDC High-fat
(approximately 800 to 1000 kCal including 55 to 65 g of fat [approximately 50% of caloric
content]). The effect of food intake on VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA PK after a single oral dose of the
FDC tablet (VNZ [Form D] 20 mg /TEZ 100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg) under fed conditions compared
to fasted conditions was statistically evaluated in Table 58. Administration of a single oral dose
of the FDC tablet following a fat containing meal increased VNZ and D-IVA exposures up to 6fold and 5-fold, respectively, compared to fasted condition. Food intake had no significant
impact on TEZ exposures overall.

Drug Interactions
Effect of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA on the PK of Each Other
The PK interactions between 10 mg VNZ [Form A], 100 mg TEZ, and 250 mg D-IVA (in
separate tablets) were evaluated in clinical trial in healthy subjects (n=20), Study VX21-121-006.
When coadministered with TEZ and D-IVA, the Cmax,ss and AUC of VNZ was increased 48%
and 54%, respectively compared to VNZ administered alone. When coadministered with VNZ
and D-IVA, TEZ Cmax,ss and AUC increased 29% and 35%, respectively, and M1-TEZ Cmax,ss
and AUC were increased 17% and 11%, respectively. Likewise, D-IVA Cmax,ss and AUC
increased 18% and 27%, respectively, M1-D-IVA Cmax,ss and AUC increased 27% and 39%,
respectively, when D-IVA was administered in TC with VNZ/TEZ compared to D-IVA
administered alone.
There is a concern whether this up to 50% increase of systemic exposures of VNZ, TEZ, D-IVA
and their active metabolites may change the efficacy/safety profile of each API in the TC
context. For VNZ, the dose ranging trial of VNZ (Study 101) was conducted in the TC context,
and the dose ranging results directly supported the 10 mg VNZ [Form A] dose in the TC context
to be used in phase 3 studies. For D-IVA, although Study 561-101 was conducted on D-IVA
mono-component in CF subjects with gating variants, based on the flat dose-response
relationship over D-IVA 150 mg to D-IVA 250 mg on ppFEV1 response observed in Study 561101, it is unlikely that the observed increase in systemic exposure would contribute additional
efficacy of D-IVA in the context of TC.
Effect of Coadministered Drugs on VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA as Substrates of CYP Enzymes
VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA are all extensively metabolized by CYP3A.
In vitro metabolizing enzyme profiling results (Study N295) showed VNZ was stable when
incubating with recombinant CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6
(>95% remaining at the 60-minute time point) as presented in Table 59. The compound exhibited
a turnover of >18% in CYP3A4 and CYP3A5 (43.2% and 81.2% remaining, respectively).

VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA showed linear PK characteristics around the therapeutic dose. Therefore,

exposures (AUC) of VNZ and D-IVA in patients on CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors (which
increase VNZ and D-IVA AUC by 2- to 5-fold) within the range of patients on normal dose
without taking CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors (Section 14.5). The extent of the proposed dose
reduction of TEZ in the context of TC when coadministered with CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors
(1/3 to 1/4) is greater than the approved dose reduction of TEZ in the context of ELX/TEZ/IVA
(1/2 of the normal dose), which is expected to result in a lower exposure (Section 14.5).
However, the dose-response relationship of TEZ was relatively flat over 30 mg to 150 mg range
when coadministered with IVA in patients with F/F, as observed in the TEZ/IVA program (Trial
VX11-661-101). Considering the availability of dosing strengths of TC and the unmet medical
need of CF, the proposed dose reduction of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA when coadministered with
CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors is reasonable.
Similarly, the proposed dose reduction to 1/10 to 1/14 of the normal dose (Table 62) is
expected to lower the VNZ and D-IVA AUC in patients on CYP3A4 strong inhibitors (which
increase VNZ and D-IVA AUC by 10.5- and 11-fold, respectively) within the range of subjects
on normal dose without taking CYP3A4 strong inhibitors (Section 14.5). Thus, the dose
reduction for VNZ and D-IVA in the context of TC when coadministered with CYP3A4 strong
inhibitors is reasonable. The extent of the proposed dose reduction of TEZ in the context of TC
when coadministered with CYP3A4 strong inhibitors (1/10 to 1/14) is greater than the approved

dose reduction of TEZ in the context of ELX/TEZ/IVA (2/7 of the normal dose). However,

The proposed pediatric dose adjustments for VNZ and D-IVA when coadministered with
CYP3A4 inhibitors are generally reasonable as population PK-simulated results showed the
AUC at steady state values after the proposed dose adjustment were within the reference range
(Section 14.5). The proposed pediatric dose adjustment for TEZ when coadministered with
CYP3A4 inhibitors generally results in a lower exposure compared to the reference range.
However, this can be similarly justified by relative flat dose-response curve of TEZ observed in
adults.
VNZ and D-IVA are sensitive substrates of CYP3A. The exposure to VNZ and D-IVA was
predicted to be reduced with concomitant use of strong or moderate CYP3A inducers. For
example, simulations showed significant decrease in exposure to VNZ and D-IVA by 82% and
90%, respectively, when coadministered with rifampin, a strong CYP3A inducer. Moreover,
carbamazepine, a strong CYP3A inducer, significantly decreased VNZ and D-IVA exposures by
57% and 76%, respectively. The exposure to VNZ and D-IVA decreased by 69% and 73%,
respectively, when coadministered with efavirenz, a moderate CYP3A inducer. Therefore,
coadministration of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet with strong or moderate CYP3A inducers is
not recommended.
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA as Substrates of Transporters
In vitro studies indicate VNZ is not a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer resistance
protein (BCRP), organic anion transporter (OAT)P1B1, and OATP1B3 (Table 63).
According to the labeling of ELX/TEZ/IVA, TEZ is a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, and OATP1B1.
Coadministration of itraconazole (CYP3A4, P-gp, and BCRP triple inhibitor) with TEZ
increased AUC and Cmax of TEZ by 4.0- and 2.8-fold, respectively, compared to TEZ
administration alone.
In vitro studies indicate D-IVA is a substrate of P-gp (efflux ratio of 6.7 at 1 µM, which is
similar to value 5.6 of IVA) and BCRP (net efflux ratio of 42.1 at 50 µM, which is similar to
value of 32.5 of IVA), but not OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 (Table 63).

Effect of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA on Other Drugs
VNZ
The plasma Cmax of VNZ at steady state following the proposed VNZ 20 mg/TEZ100 mg/D-IVA
250 mg is around 1.35 µM. That translates to about ≤20 nM free Cmax plasma concentration of
VNZ. In vitro results showed that VNZ did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and
CYP3A4, and showed weak inhibition of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP2C9 with the most potent
Ki value as 14.2 µM (for CYP2B6). The half maximal effective concentration value of VNZ on
CYP3A4 mRNA induction was estimated ranging from 0.3 to 1.4 μM, and an Emax ranging from
3.4- to 5.2-fold. Comparison of in vivo Cmax value and in vitro inhibition/induction values, the in
vivo CYP inhibition/induction potential of VNZ is low.
VNZ is a P-gp inhibitor with a half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) of 3.80 μM when
digoxin is used as substrate. The calculated induced gut/IC50 value is 35. Given that
coadministration of VNZ with TEZ (a P-gp substrate) and D-IVA (a weak P-gp inhibitor) only
increased TEZ Cmax and AUC by 29% and 35%, VNZ is likely having low potential to inhibit
P-gp in vivo.
VNZ is a BCRP inhibitor with an IC50 of 0.25 μM when prazosin is used as a substrate. The
calculated induced gut/IC50 value is 532, which is almost two magnitudes higher than the cutoff
of 10, suggesting VNZ has a potential to inhibit BCRP in vivo. Therefore, a postmarketing
requirement study to evaluate the effect of VNZ or VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA on PK of a sensitive BCRP
substrate is recommended.
VNZ inhibited OATP1B1- and OATP1B3-mediated atorvastatin uptake with IC50 of 45.1 and
61.7 μM, respectively, in the presence of HBE medium which contained 20% human serum. The
113

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
calculated IC50,u/Cmax,inlet,u (1.47 μM) is smaller than the cutoff value of 10. Therefore, the in vivo
OATP1B1- and OATP1B3 inhibition potential of VNZ is low.
TEZ
Refer to the approved drug label of ELX/TEZ/IVA (Vertex Pharmaceuticals 2019), in vitro
results showed that TEZ has low potential to inhibit all the major CYP enzymes. TEZ is not
likely to induce CYP3A, CYP1A2 and CYP2B6. TEZ has low potential to inhibit P-gp, BCRP,
organic cation transporter 2, OAT1, or OAT3.
D-IVA
Major CYP enzymes inhibition data for D-IVA, M1-D-IVA and M6-D-IVA are summarized in
Table 65. In general, CYP3A4, CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inhibition potencies are similar between
IVA and D-IVA, M1-IVA and M1-D-IVA.
Therefore, it is expected that the in vivo CYP3A4, CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inhibition potential by
D-IVA is similar to slightly higher than that of IVA because of the doubling of Cmax value. Of
note, in clinical studies, IVA did not significantly affect the PK of rosiglitazone, a model
CYP2C8 substrate, and midazolam, a sensitive CYP3A substrate (Study 770-010 and Study 661(b) (4)
006).
(i.e., D-IVA may inhibit CYP2C9; therefore, monitoring of the
international normalized ratio during co administration of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA with warfarin is
recommended). Other medicinal products for which exposure may be increased by VNZ/TEZ/DIVA include glimepiride and glipizide; these medicinal products should be used with caution.

Following individual incubation with increasing concentrations of D-IVA, M1-D-IVA, or M6-DIVA for 72 hours with plated human hepatocytes, less than 20% (positive threshold) increase of

In general, the P-gp and BCRP inhibition potencies are similar between IVA and D-IVA, M6IVA and M6-D-IVA. M1-D-IVA is less potent to inhibit P-gp and BCRP than M1-IVA.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
CF is a rare, orphan disease and is not subject to pediatric study requirements as defined under
the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA). However, the Applicant submitted proposals for the
inclusion of pediatric subjects within the clinical development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA,
and a Pediatric Written Request was issued on December 6, 2023.
Following approval, the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA will include patients aged 6 years and
older. This is based on safety and efficacy data obtained from the phase 3 noninferiority trials,
Trials 102 and 103, which enrolled subjects 12 to 17 years of age (reviewed in Sections 6.2.2,
6.2.3, and 7.6.1). Additionally, a pediatric PK and safety trial, Trial 105, is ongoing to evaluate
the PK of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA when dosed in triple combination in CF subjects 1 through 11 years
of age (inclusive) with at least one TCR variant in the CFTR gene. In this application, the
Applicant submitted interim analysis results using data from subjects 6 through 11 years of age
in Cohorts A1 and B1 to support the extrapolation of efficacy and safety in this population
(reviewed in Sections 6.2.4 and 7.6.2). The remaining cohorts in younger CF subjects 1-5 years
of age (Cohorts A2, A3, B2, and B3) are ongoing.
(b) (4)

juvenile toxicology studies were not needed to support
this application for subjects aged 6 years and older. In a juvenile rat toxicology study,
vanzacaftor had no adverse effects at up to 58 (male)/28 (female) times the exposure at the
MRHD. Juvenile animal toxicology studies for IVA and TEZ were previously reviewed under
NDA 203188 and NDA 212273, respectively.

9. Product Quality
Approval
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed NDA 218730 with respect to
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all applicable
standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such Office of
Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
Drug Substance
The CMC information for two of the APIs, VNZ and D-IVA, is provided in this NDA (NDA
218730). For the remaining API, TEZ, the Applicant has fully cross-referenced their own
approved NDA 210491. Four minor issues involving VNZ and D-IVA were identified via our
original review of the NDA on September 13, 2024. However, the Applicant’s responses on
September 20, 2024, have resolved those issues. Thus, the NDA is currently recommended for
approval from the API perspectives.
Drug Product
Vertex developed the drug product manufacturing process for an immediate-release, oral
(b) (4)
formulation. The process involves
as outlined in Section 2.3.P.3. A Quality by Design approach was utilized to identify
critical quality attributes for efficacy and safety. In-process control and release testing was
implemented to monitor these critical quality attributes and ensure consistent product quality.
The drug product specifications were established using a Quality by Design approach,
identifying critical quality attributes and referencing international harmonization guidelines and
United States Pharmacopeia chapters. Data from development, release testing, stability studies,
and manufacturing, were used to establish the specifications. The established specifications
ensure the drug product's identity, strength, purity, and overall quality throughout its intended
24-month shelf life.
The noncompendial analytical methods ensure the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the
drug product at release and throughout its shelf-life. These methods have been validated
according to International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) Q2(R1) guidelines.

Biopharmaceutics
From a biopharmaceutics perspective, the application is adequate, and approval is recommended.
The to-be-marketed and phase 3 formulations were the same so there was no need for bridging.
Based on information provided, the Applicant considers the VNZ and TEZ to be
Biopharmaceutical Classification System Class 2 (low solubility/high permeability). D-IVA is
considered to be either Biopharmaceutical Classification System Class 2 or 4, as permeability
studies were inconclusive. No biowaiver has been requested and both strength combinations
were used in the phase 3 clinical study. The Applicant has provided data demonstrating that the
dissolution methods are sensitive to material attributes, minor changes in the tablets’
composition, as well as variation in the process parameters that could influence the release of
VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA. The methods are considered to be suitable for regulatory purposes. The
Applicant has tightened the dissolution acceptance criteria for VNZ and TEZ to be reflective of
the associated data and all dissolution acceptance criteria are now adequate.

to ongoing negotiations with the Applicant. There are only minor labeling deficiencies from the
CMC perspective, and we expect the Applicant will accept our recommendations. Refer to the
CMC sections of the final approved labeling.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
Tezacaftor (TEZ, VX-661)
Tezacaftor was approved on February 12, 2018, as part of new drug application (NDA) 210491
(TEZ/IVA). Vertex submitted a new molecular entity NDA on June 28, 2017, for TEZ/IVA
combination therapy “for the treatment for patients with cystic fibrosis (CF) aged 12 years and
older who are homozygous for the F508del (F) mutation or who have at least one mutation in the
cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator (CFTR) gene that is responsive to TEZ/IVA
based on in vitro data and/or clinical evidence.”
Deutivacaftor (D-IVA, CTP-656)
On December 22, 2015, a Pre-investigational new drug meeting request was received to discuss
the plans for CTP-656, a deuterated isotopolog of ivacaftor (IVA) for use in CF patients. In the
meeting minutes dated March 11, 2016, the Division provided clinical and clinical pharmacology
comments regarding the development program, the comparator arms and wash-out period and
stated that the proposed phase 2 study was insufficient to support phase 3 dosing. The Division
also noted that the extent of the nonclinical and clinical bridge between D-IVA and IVA was
unclear and the need for additional nonclinical studies would be determined by the adequacy of
the bridging program.
On November 18, 2016, investigational new drug (IND) 128544 was submitted, for D-IVA, as a
once-daily dosing treatment for patients with CF. The submission included phase 1 studies: a
single ascending dose study, a multiple ascending dose study, and a bioavailability/food effect
study conducted in Australia. The first study under the IND was a phase 2 study in which three
doses of D-IVA, placebo, or open-label KALYDECO (IVA) were administered for 28 days. The
study was deemed safe to proceed on December 23, 2016, with nonhold comments.
Vertex submitted a chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) Type C meeting request on
August 14, 2017, for D-IVA, being developed in combination with TEZ and either VX-659 or
ELX, to discuss an approach to leveraging existing CMC information for IVA to support
development of D-IVA. Meeting Minutes were issued on October 18, 2017.
On September 19, 2018, the Division provided feedback for the submission received on July 24,
2018, on the proposed design of a phase 2 study evaluating a range of doses for D-IVA and the
commercial dose of IVA. The comments discussed concerns that the proposed dose ranging
study alone would not be sufficient to support phase 3 dosing.
Vanzacaftor
On April 10, 2019, IND 142001 for vanzacaftor (VNZ), a new molecular entity, was submitted
with the proposed protocol, VX18-121-101 (Study 101) entitled “A Phase 2, Randomized,
Double-blind, Controlled Study to Evaluate the Safety and Efficacy of VX-121 [VNZ]
122
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Combination Therapy in Subjects Aged 18 Years and Older With Cystic Fibrosis.” The
submission also included a request for Fast Track designation. The IND was granted Fast Track
designation and deemed safe to proceed on May 30, 2019.
Rare Pediatric Disease Designation was granted on October 1, 2020 (RPD-2020-485). On
October 26, 2020, Orphan Designation (DRU-2020-772) was granted for VNZ for the treatment
of CF.
A Type B end-of-phase 2 meeting request dated December 9, 2020, was submitted to gain
agreement on the proposed pivotal clinical development program and to discuss the adequacy of
the nonclinical, clinical, and clinical pharmacology data to support initiation of phase 3 studies,
as well as an NDA submission for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. In the meeting minutes dated March 26,
2021, the following discussions occurred:
•

The Division advised Vertex to conduct a dedicated crossover pharmacokinetic (PK) study
with four treatment arms (VNZ, TEZ, D-IVA, and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA) to evaluate the PK
interaction potential among the three mono-components before initiation of phase 3 trials,
further noting that evaluation of the drug-drug interactions (DDIs) between individual
components of a combination product is a required step to satisfy the “combination rule”
(21 CFR 300.50).

•

The Division raised a concern for the proposed dose for D-IVA, noting the current safety
database for D-IVA was limited, and safety for D-IVA should not be borrowed from the IVA
program. The Division strongly advised that the safety database should consist of 52 weekcontrolled data for more complete evaluation of safety.

•

The Division acknowledged the justification for noninferiority margin using a meta-analytic
approach and noted that substitution of D-IVA for IVA adds uncertainty for indirect
comparison. The Division recommend that Vertex submit further biological or clinical
justification for the proposed margin.

On April 26, 2021, a Type C meeting request was submitted to discuss Vertex’s approach in
demonstrating the contribution of the individual components of the fixed-dose combination
therapy VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and the design of 3-way crossover study to evaluate the PK
interaction potential among VNZ, TEZ/D-IVA, and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The Preliminary
Comments issued on August 12, 2021, noted that final determination of satisfying the
combination rule would depend on the strength of the D-IVA to IVA bridge and that although
the proposed 3-period crossover study design for the PK DDI study for individual components
was reasonable, the waiver of a DDI study between TEZ and D-IVA should be sufficiently
justified. On July 30, 2021, the Division also provided comments on the phase 3 trial protocols
submitted on July 8, 2021.
Breakthrough designation request was received on May 27, 2021, and was denied on July 20,
2021, because the data with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was limited by small sample size, short study
duration, and lack of concurrent standard of care comparison and because the data did not show a
substantial improvement over existing therapies.
On October 7, 2021, a Type C meeting request was submitted to discuss the planned pediatric
development program, seeking agreement on the proposed study design and the data to support

mutation in the CFTR gene.”

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
The Applicant has conducted a comprehensive program of pharmacology, pharmacokinetic, and
toxicology studies to support clinical development and the NDA submission of VNZ/TEZ/DIVA. VNZ is the only component of this triple combination (TC) for which a complete
nonclinical program was needed. All pivotal toxicology studies of VNZ have been submitted and
reviewed previously under IND 142001. The Applicant performed a bridging study between DIVA and IVA to leverage existing IVA nonclinical data. The Applicant has also provided
combination toxicology studies for the new TC. The impurities studies have been submitted and
reviewed under this application. The key findings from the nonclinical studies are cited and
summarized in this review to support the approval of NDA 218730.

13.1.1. Pharmacology
Primary pharmacology studies using human bronchial epithelial (HBE) cells from CF donors
demonstrated that VNZ was able to improve CFTR glycosylation which would be expected to
improve CFTR trafficking to the membrane. VNZ alone did not greatly improve CFTR function
as evaluated by chloride (Cl) transport. However, VNZ in combination with TEZ and IVA
greatly enhanced CFTR activity. VNZ did not appear to act as a potentiator for CFTR.
Secondary pharmacology studies found that VNZ inhibits human prostanoid EP4 (Ki 0.64 μM),
IP (PGI2) (Ki 0.83 μM), prostanoid EP3 (Ki 1.27 μM), prostanoid EP2 (Ki 3.23 μM), rat
adrenergic α1A (Ki 3.48 μM), and rat adrenergic α1B (Ki 7.46 μM).

13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
Safety pharmacology studies indicated that VNZ does not affect the CNS or respiratory systems
in rats at doses up to 10 mg/kg. In a good laboratory practice (GLP) human ether-a-go-go related
gene assay, VNZ inhibited the channel with a half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) of 0.7
μM. The safety pharmacology study evaluating cardiovascular toxicity in dogs did not note any
treatment-related effects. Additionally, the 4-week and 39-week repeat-dose toxicology studies
in dogs evaluated electrocardiogram (ECG) parameters and did not find any treatment-related
effects. Therefore, the results of the human ether-a-go-go related gene assay were not considered
clinically meaningful.
Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion/PK
Metabolite profiling using liver microsomes and cryopreserved hepatocytes of human, rat, dog,
and monkey origin did not identify any human-specific metabolites. There were also no major
metabolites identified in rats, dogs, or monkeys. Cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4/5 appeared to be
the major metabolic enzymes responsible for VNZ metabolism via oxidative metabolism. VNZ
does not appear to partition into rat, dog, monkey, or human red blood cells. Plasma binding was
high (>99.9% bound) across rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans. Tissue distribution study using
125
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
male Sprague-Dawley rats given single oral doses of VNZ (3, 10, and 30 mg/kg), found little
drug distribution to the brain, lung, heart, and kidney (tissue to plasma ratios ranged from 0.1151.18 and did not increase with dose). Liver to plasma ratios were higher than all other tissues and
decreased with increasing dose (17.3, 11.53, and 3.16, respectively).

13.1.3. General Toxicology
Repeat Dose Toxicology Studies In Rats
4-Week Rat Study (VRT-1725010-TX-006)
In a GLP study, Sprague Dawley rats (10 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral
gavage once daily at 0 (vehicle), 1, 5, and 20 mg/kg/day in males and at 0, 0.5, 2.5, and
10 mg/kg/day in females for 4 weeks. The sex-specific dosing was due to preliminary studies
showing that female rats had twice the exposure of male rats receiving the same dose. There was
one low dose female that was euthanized on Day 7 due to shallow, difficult breathing. The cause
of death was likely from technical error with the gavage delivery. Hematology noted a dosedependent decrease in basophil counts in both males and females. Monocyte counts increased in
a dose-dependent manner for both sexes. No potential target organs of toxicity were identified.
The no observed adverse effect level (NOAELs) were identified as 20 mg/kg (AUC:
418 hr∙μg/mL) for males and 10 mg/kg (AUC: 655 hr∙μg/mL) for females.
26-Week Rat Study (VX-121-TX-007)
In a GLP study, Sprague Dawley rats (15 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral
gavage once daily at 0 (vehicle), 5, 12, 25, and 125 mg/kg/day in males and 0, 2.5, 6, 12.5, and
30 mg/kg/day in females for 26 weeks with additional animals included in the control and
125(male)/30(female) mg/kg/day groups for a 6-week recovery period. All male rats
administered 125 mg/kg/day VNZ were found dead or euthanized by Day 14 of the study due to
declining clinical condition. Most of the animals had erosions or ulcers in the nonglandular or
glandular stomach. Four female rats in the main and toxicokinetic groups administered
30 mg/kg/day VNZ were euthanized or found dead on Day 98, 136 (2), and 183. A cause of
death was not identified for all of the female rats but several had findings in the stomach. Target
organs of toxicity were the liver (hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration for males at 25 and
125 mg/kg/day and multifocal hepatocellular necrosis for females at the 30 mg/kg/day),
glandular stomach (erosion(s) and severe ulceration for males at 125 mg/kg/day and females at
30 mg/kg/day), nonglandular stomach (ulceration for males at 125 mg/kg/day, hyperplasia and/or
hyperkeratosis in males at 125 mg/kg/day and females at 30 mg/kg/day), adrenal gland
(hypertrophy for males ≥25 mg/kg/day and females ≥12.5 mg/kg/day), and decreases in
lymphocytes in the spleen, thymus, and lymph nodes for males ≥25 mg/kg/day and females
≥12.5 mg/kg/day. The NOAELs were identified as 12 mg/kg/day (AUC: 390 μg∙hr/mL) for
males and 12.5 mg/kg/day (AUC: 1120 μg∙hr/mL) for females due to mortality, reduced body
weight, multifocal hepatocellular necrosis, ulcers and/or erosions in the glandular and
nonglandular stomach for males at 125 mg/kg/day and females at 30 mg/kg/day, and
hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration for males at 25 and 125 mg/kg/day.

ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Repeat Dose Toxicology Studies In Beagle Dogs
4-Week Dog Study (VRT-1725010-TX-007)
In a GLP study, Beagle dogs (4 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral gavage once
daily at 0 (vehicle), 0.3, 1.0, and 4.0 mg/kg for 4 weeks. One high dose female was euthanized
on Day 11 due to clinical signs of coughing, lacrimation, decreased activity, emesis with frothy
white material that progressed to frothy red material, and red frothy vomitus. At necropsy, the
lungs were discolored red which correlated microscopically with hemorrhage. There was also
moderate edema and neutrophilic infiltration. These findings were consistent with aspiration of
the test article and likely due to a gavage error. There were no adverse findings in the remaining
animals at the end of the study. No potential target organs of toxicity were identified. The
NOAEL identified as 4.0 mg/kg (AUC: 356 μg∙hr/mL, average of males and females).
39-Week Dog Study (VX-121-TX-008)
In a GLP study, Beagle dogs (4 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral gavage once
daily at 0 (vehicle), 1.5, 4, or 10 mg/kg for 39 weeks with additional animals included in the
control and 10 mg/kg/day groups for a 6-week recovery period. All dogs were sexually mature
based on histopathologic assessment. No potential target organs of toxicity were identified. The
NOAEL was identified as 10 mg/kg/day (AUC: 1960 μg∙hr/mL, average of males and females).
Special Studies
13-Week Oral Rat Bridging Study for D-IVA
A 13-week oral rat bridging study (VX-561-TX-001) was conducted to compare D-IVA at 0
(vehicle), 8.75, 17.5, 35, or 100 mg/kg/day to IVA at 100 mg/kg/day only. Unscheduled deaths
in 19% of 100 mg/kg D-IVA and 11% of 100 mg/kg IVA animals resulted in early termination
of both 100 mg/kg groups one week early. Target organs were identified as the kidney
(basophilic tubules, tubular dilatation, and interstitial fibrosis), stomach (increased mucosal
thickness), and thyroid (follicular cell hypertrophy). There were no toxicologically significant
differences in clinical pathology, organ weights, or histopathology at 100 mg/kg/day D-IVA
versus IVA. There were no adverse findings at the lower D-IVA doses. The NOAEL was
identified as 35 mg/kg/day D-IVA. This study was judged adequate to establish a bridge to the
ivacaftor nonclinical program to support the clinical development of D-IVA. No further general
toxicology, developmental and reproductive toxicology, or carcinogenicity studies were
recommended.
13-Week Combination Rat Study (VX-121-TX-012/VX-661-TX-088/VX-561-TX-001)
In a GLP study, Sprague Dawley rats (10 animals/sex/group) were administered 0 (vehicle), VNZ (2.5 mg/kg/day) alone or in dual or TC with TEZ (45 mg/kg/day) and/or D-IVA
(17.5 mg/kg/day) by oral gavage once daily for 13 weeks. TEZ- and VNZ/D-IVA/TEZ-related
clinical chemistry (decreases in triglyceride concentrations) and urinalysis effects (increases in
urine volume with concurrent decreases in urine specific gravity) were observed in males. No
potential target organs of toxicity were identified. There were no additive toxicity effects when
VNZ was administered in combinations with TEZ and/or D-IVA. The NOAELs were identified

13.1.4. Genetic Toxicology
Vanzacaftor was negative in the Ames assay, in vitro micronucleus assay in TK6 cells, and in
vivo micronucleus assay in male Sprague-Dawley rats.
Carcinogenicity
In the 6-month carcinogenicity study, transgenic rasH2 mice were administered 0 (vehicle), 3,
10, and 30 mg/kg/day vanzacaftor by oral gavage. The Applicant did have concurrence from the
Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee for dose selection. There were no treatmentrelated effects on mortality rates and body weights. There were no statistically significant
treatment-related tumor findings in male or female mice. All animals in the positive control
group (urethane) were observed with neoplastic findings. Non-neoplastic target organs of
toxicity were kidney (multifocal unilateral mononuclear cell infiltration) and thymus (minimal
hemorrhage).
Developmental and Reproductive Toxicology
Fertility Studies
In a GLP fertility study (Study VX-121-TX-009), VNZ was administrated to rats at oral doses of
0 (vehicle), 2.5, 5, and 12.5 (Male, M)/10 (Female, F) mg/kg/day (3, 9, and 19 (M)/30 (F) times
the exposure at the maximum recommended human dose [MRHD], respectively). Males were
dosed for 28 days prior to mating, throughout mating, and continuing until the day prior to
euthanasia. Females were dosed for 14 days prior to mating, throughout mating, and continuing
until Gestation Day 7. VNZ had no effects on male and female fertility and reproductive
performance indices up to 12.5 (M)/10 (F) mg/kg/day (19 (M)/30 (F) times the exposure at the
128
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
MRHD). In conclusion, the NOAEL for fertility in this study was determined to be 12.5 mg/kg
in males and 10 mg/kg in females associated with AUC0­24h of 361 and 565 µg·hr/mL,
respectively.
Embryofetal Development Studies
In a GLP embryo-fetal development study (Study VX-121-TX-005), pregnant rats were
administered VNZ at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 2.5, 5, and 10 mg/kg/day (4, 10, and 30 times the
exposure at the MRHD, respectively) during the period of organogenesis from gestation days 6
to 17. VNZ did not cause adverse effects to the fetus at exposures up to 30 times the MRHD. In
conclusion, the NOAEL for maternal and embryo/fetal toxicities was determined to be
10 mg/kg/day (AUC0­24h, 565 µg·hr/mL) in rats.
In a GLP embryo-fetal development study (Study VX-121-TX-006), pregnant rabbits were
administered VNZ at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 10, 40, and 70 mg/kg/day (3, 22, and 71 times the
exposure at the MRHD, respectively) during the period of organogenesis from gestation Days 7
to 20. VNZ did not cause adverse effects to the fetus at maternal exposures up to 22 times the
MRHD. The high dose of 70 mg/kg/day (71 times the exposure at the MRHD) produced
maternal toxicity (i.e., mortality, abortion, decreased mean body weight or body weight gains)
and was associated with findings of increased postimplantation loss, decreased live fetuses,
decreased fetal body weight, increased kidney malformations, and increased incidences of
skeletal variations. The effects observed at the high dose were related to maternal toxicity and so
were not considered relevant to the human risk. In conclusion, the NOAEL for maternal and
embryo/fetal toxicities was determined to be 40 mg/kg/day (AUC0­24h, 410 µg·hr/mL) in rabbits.
Pre- and Postnatal Development Study
In a GLP pre- and postnatal development study (Study VX-121-TX-017), pregnant rats were
administered VNZ at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 2.5, 5, and 10 mg/kg/day (approximately 2, 7, and
18 times the exposure at the MRHD, respectively) from gestation Day 7 to lactation/postpartum
Day 20. No adverse effects were observed in the offspring at maternal doses up to approximately
18 times the MRHD. On PND 14, AUC values of VNZ in dams were 10.0 to 28.6 times greater
than that in pups. In conclusion, the NOAEL for maternal and developmental toxicities was
determined to be 10 mg/kg/day (AUC0­24h, 339 µg·hr/mL) in rats.
Juvenile Animal Study
In a GLP juvenile animal study (Study VX-121-TX-019), juvenile rats were administered VNZ
at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 2.5, 10 (M)/6 (F), and 25 (M)/12.5 (F) mg/kg/day (approximately 4,
15, and 58 (M)/ 28 (F) times the exposure at the MRHD, respectively) from postnatal Day 7
through postnatal Day 70. No adverse effects were observed at oral doses up to approximately 58
(M)/28 (F) times the MRHD. Increased in white blood cell count, gamma-glutamyl transferase
activity, total bilirubin, total bile acids, and phosphorus concentrations and decreased cholesterol
and urea were observed in a dose-dependent manner in VNZ-treated groups. Without associated
histopathological findings, these findings are not considered adverse. In conclusion, the NOAEL
for juvenile toxicities was determined to be 25 mg/kg/day in males and 12.5 mg/kg/day in
females, associated with AUC0­24h of 1100 and 532 µg·hr/mL, respectively.

3.2.S.3.2 Impurities
The Applicant followed the recommendations of the International Council for Harmonisation
(ICH) guidance for industry M7(R1) Assessment and Control of DNA Reactive (Mutagenic)
Impurities in Pharmaceuticals to Limit Potential Carcinogenic Risk (March 2018) and evaluated
the mutagenic potential of impurities based on literature, internal databases, and in silico
assessment including Leadscope Genetox Statistical Models and Derek Nexus. Overall, the
proposed impurity specifications are acceptable and there are no outstanding nonclinical safety
concerns.
Other identified impurities are appropriately controlled per the ICH guidance for industry
Q3A(R) Impurities in New Drug Substances (June 2008), ICH guidance for industry Q3B(R)
Impurities in New Drug Products (August 2006), United States Pharmacopeia (USP) Guidelines
or qualified in nonclinical toxicology studies.

Deutivacaftor
Absorption

Conclusions
Blood to plasma concentration ratio of VNZ (1 μM) in human was
0.588 (N309) and 0.513 (N277).
VNZ exhibited high binding to plasma proteins with average
percent bound values of 99.99% for human.
In vitro, VNZ binds more extensively to human serum albumin
(HSA) than α1­acid glycoprotein (AAG), suggesting that HSA is
likely the major human plasma protein for VNZ binding and AAG
plays a minor role.
To estimate the free concentration in the HBE assay, protein
binding of VNZ in HBE medium containing 20% human serum
was evaluated by equilibrium dialysis. The percent bound value of
VNZ in HBE medium containing 20% human serum was 99.89%.
VX121 is stable (>85% remaining) when incubating with CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6. The
compound shows greater turnover in CYP3A4 (43% remaining)
and CYP3A5 (81% remaining), suggesting CYP3A isozymes are
primarily responsible for VNZ metabolism.
In vitro metabolite profiles were qualitatively similar across
human, mouse, rat, dog, and monkey. No human-specific
metabolites were observed in vitro.
Metabolic profile of 14C-VX121 was investigated in plasma, urine,
and feces samples from the mass balance Study 004. See
individual study report for Study 004 for summary of results.
VNZ is predicted to have a low potential for CYP3A4 induction at
clinically relevant concentrations.
The IC50 values of VNZ for inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, and CYP3A, were all greater than 30 µM, suggesting
low potential for inhibition of these enzymes by VNZ.
Weak inhibition of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP2C9 was
observed (IC50=14.2, 18.1, and 18.4 μM, respectively).
Pre-incubation of VNZ with human liver microsomes in the
presence of NADPH indicated the potential of VNZ to inactivate
CYP3A4/5 in a time-dependent manner was low.
VNZ is not a substrate of human OATP1B1 or OATP1B3.
VNZ is neither a human P-gp substrate nor a human BCRP
substrate.
VNZ is a P-gp inhibitor with an IC50 of 3.80 μM.
VNZ has a potential to inhibit BCRP in vivo (IC50=0.252 μM).
VNZ has low potential to inhibit OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 in vivo
at the therapeutic dose.
VNZ is not expected to cause clinically relevant drug interactions
via inhibition of OAT1 in vivo.
The potential of VNZ to inhibit OAT3 transporter in the clinic is
predicted to be low.
No concentration dependent inhibition of OCT1/2 and MATE1/2-K
by VNZ was observed in vitro with IC50 values >81 μM.
The IC50 for the inhibitory effect of VNZ on hERG potassium
current was 0.7 μM (Hill coefficient =1.5), which provides >~3800fold margin over the predicted free Cmax at the projected
therapeutic dose.

Conclusions
An in vitro study showed that blood to plasma concentration ratio
in human was 0.612-0.738 and 0.869-0.902 for D-IVA and M1-DIVA, respectively.
D-IVA and M1-D-IVA exhibited high binding to human plasma
protein, with average percent bound values of 99.93% and
99.24%, respectively.
In vitro, D-IVA and M1-D-IVA bind more extensively to HSA than
AAG, suggesting that HSA is likely the major human plasma
protein for D-IVA and M1-D-IVA binding and AAG plays a minor
role.
D-IVA was stable (>85% remaining) when incubating with
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 and CYP2D6.
D-IVA showed greater turnover in CYP3A4 (6.00% remaining),
and CYP3A5 (18.6% remaining), indicating that CYP3A isozymes
are primarily responsible for D-IVA metabolism.
Metabolism of D-IVA in human liver microsomes and human
hepatocytes involved oxidation of the deuterated tert-butyl to form
M1-D-IVA and its sequential metabolite, M6-D-IVA.
D-IVA exhibited an approximately 2.5 times longer t1/2 than IVA
(12.2 vs 4.8 minutes) in human recombinant CYP3A4 in vitro.
D-IVA, M1-D-IVA, and M6-D-IVA are predicted to have low
induction potential for CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4.
In vitro inhibition data indicate low potential for inhibition of
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6 by D-IVA, M1-D-IVA,
and M6-D-IVA.
D-IVA and M1-D-IVA have comparable inhibition potency of
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A.
The potential of D-IVA to inactivate CYP3A4/5 in a timedependent manner was low.
D-IVA and M1-D-IVA are not substrates of OATP1B1 or
OATP1B3, but they are substrates of P-gp and BCRP, whereas
M6-D-IVA is a substrate of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and BCRP but
not a substrate of P-gp.
D-IVA and M1-D-IVA inhibits P-gp with IC50s of 3.83 and 9.86 µM,
respectively. D-IVA and M1-D-IVA inhibits BCRP with IC50s of
1.19 and 9.15 µM, respectively.
No dose-dependent inhibition of OATP1B1/3, OAT1/3, OCT1/2,
and MATE1/2-K, by D-IVA, M1-D-IVA and M6-D-IVA was
observed with all IC50 values >100 μM.
D-IVA, M1-D-IVA and M6-D-IVA had a weak inhibition of BSEP in
vitro with IC50 values >10 μM.
D-IVA inhibited hERG current with an IC20 of 0.86 μM, which
provides >200 fold margin of exposure over the proposed
therapeutic dose of 250 mg QD.

14.1.2. In Vitro Electrophysiology Studies
Regulatory History
There is regulatory precedent for using in vitro data from the Ussing chamber assays to support
expansion of approval for IVA (NDA 203188 KALYDECO), TEZ/IVA (NDA 210491
SYMDEKO), and ELX/TEZ/IVA (NDA 212273 TRIKAFTA) to CFTR variants when the
clinical data for that variant was not available. For the combination products, Ussing chamber
data have also been leveraged to understand the contribution of each drug component to the
overall treatment effect.
For IVA, the Applicant has previously provided Ussing chamber data obtained from CF patientderived primary HBE cells and FRT cells genetically engineered to express different variant
forms of human CFTR proteins. The Ussing chamber data for supporting TEZ/IVA and
ELX/TEZ/IVA were generated from FRT cell lines mostly, and from HBE cells for limited
variants (i.e., F508del/F508del, F508del/minimal function (MF), N1303K/N1303K, and
N1303K/MF).
The Applicant also submitted in vitro Western Blot data to demonstrate the treatment effect on
the level of mature CFTR proteins delivered to the cell membrane. Western Blot data are useful
to understand the mechanism-of-action of TEZ, ELX, and VNZ, which affect processing and
trafficking dysfunctions. However, Western Blot data have not been used to identify whether a
CFTR variant is a “responder” to treatment. For this purpose, direct measurement of chloride
transport using the Ussing chamber assay is used.
Background for the Current Application
The currently approved triple combination therapy for CF patients carrying treatment-responsive
CFTR variants is ELX/TEZ/IVA. NDA 218730 is for a new triple combination that includes a
new molecular entity VNZ to replace ELX, and a deuterated isotopologue of IVA (D-IVA) to
replace IVA. VNZ performs the same function as ELX to help processing and trafficking of
mutant CFTR proteins, thereby increasing their surface expression. D-IVA performs the same
function as IVA to increase the amount of time that CFTR channels stay in the open state,
thereby allowing more ions to pass through. The Applicant has identified 303 variants to be
responsive to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, based on the following evidence or rationale:
Clinical Data (24 Variants Total)
•

Twenty-four variants, including F508del and 23 other triple combination-responsive variants
are supported with clinical data. These variants are already indicated for ELX/TEZ/IVA.

133
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
In Vitro FRT Data (257 Variants Total)
•

One hundred and twenty-nine (129) variants respond to TEZ/IVA or IVA. These have not
been tested with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA directly. The rationale that they will respond to
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is based on data that shows functional equivalence of D-IVA and IVA,
and the idea that further functional correction, hence additional clinical benefit, can be
expected by the additional drug components in the new triple combination.

•

Seventy-four (74) variants that were recently shown to respond to ELX/TEZ/IVA (refer to
the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 & 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December
11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]; source: U032) are now shown to respond to VNZ/TEZ/DIVA (source: U015).

•

Twenty-tree (23) variants that were previously shown to respond to ELX/TEZ/IVA (source:
P289v4) but not to TEZ/IVA or IVA are now shown to respond to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (source:
U015).

•

Thirty-one (31) variants did not respond to ELX/TEZ/IVA. These are now shown to respond
to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (source: U015).

In Vitro HBE Data (One Variant: N1303K)
•

N1303K did not respond to ELX/TEZ/IVA when expressed in the FRT system. HBE cells
from homozygous and heterozygous N1303K CF donors (the latter with minimal function on
the 2nd allele not expected to produce CFTR proteins) are used to demonstrate positive
response to ELX/TEZ/IVA and VNZ/TEZ-D-IVA through comparisons with data from
F508del/F508del and F508del/MF HBE cells in response to TEZ/IVA treatment (source:
U020 and S525).

Splice Variants (19 Variants Total)
•

The rationale for including these noncanonical splice (NCS) variants is that clinical data
showed that these variants have phenotype of residual function (RF) (interpreted as the
presence of a small number of wild-type CFTR proteins in these subjects) and in vitro FRT
data demonstrating VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA increased chloride transport on wild-type CFTR
proteins (source: U015).

In Vitro Electrophysiology Data That Directly Compared the Effects of D-IVA and IVA on
Chloride Transport
For D-IVA, deuterium is substituted with nine hydrogens at the primary site of metabolism for
(b) (4)
IVA (on the tert-butyl group adjacent to the hydroxyl group of the phenol)
This strategy is based on the rationale that even though deuterium has similar chemical
properties as hydrogen, carbon-deuterium bonds are stronger than corresponding carbon(b) (4)
hydrogen bonds.

134

Five pieces of in vitro electrophysiology data directly compared the effects of D-IVA and IVA
on increasing CFTR-mediated chloride transport. These data are derived from the following
experiments and study reports:
For experiments conducted using HBE cells from CF donors
•

G551D/F508del-HBE cells treated with D-IVA or IVA (source: PH-088)

•

F508del/F508del-HBE cells pretreated with lumacaftor and then acutely treated with D-IVA
or IVA (source PH-088)

•

F508del/F508del-HBE cells treated with D-IVA or IVA (source: PH-043)

•

F508del/minimal function variant treated with D-IVA or IVA, in the presence of VNZ/TEZ
(source: Q142)

For experiments conducted using FRT cells expressing human CFTR variants
•

G551D CFTR variant treated with D-IVA or IVA (source: PH-045)

Results from these experiments showed that D-IVA and IVA have similar potencies on
increasing CFTR-mediated current or produced similar magnitude of CFTR-mediated chloride
transport, supporting the functional equivalence of D-IVA and IVA. The variants studied in these
experiments are carried by approximately 85% of CF patients.
In Vitro Electrophysiology Data That Demonstrated Contributions of Individual Drug
Components to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA Triple Combination
Per 21 CFR 300.50(a), “two or more drugs may be combined in a single dosage form when each
component makes a contribution to the claimed effect and the dosage of each…is such that the
combination is safe and effective for a significant patient population requiring such concurrent
therapy…” Two pieces of in vitro electrophysiology data were submitted by the Applicant that
can be used to support the benefit of individual drug components in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
combination. Figure 5 below shows experiments conducted on HBE cells from CF donors
carrying F508del/F508del or F508del/MF variant. VNZ was tested across multiple
concentrations alone, in the presence of TEZ (18 µM), in the presence of D-IVA (1 µM), or in
the presence of both TEZ/D-IVA. Comparing the magnitudes of chloride transport at the vertical
dotted lines in this figure (which correspond to VNZ plasma concentration following an
efficacious dose in a phase 2 study), the following order of treatment efficacy can be derived
from the comparison of their in vitro potency (from highest to lowest): VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA >
VNZ/D-IVA > VNZ/TEZ > VNZ. Note that the TEZ/IVA single-concentration (i.e., 18 µM/1
µM) data are displayed to the lower left corner for reference. Given that IVA and D-IVA are
demonstrated to be functionally equivalent (see section above), the potency of TEZ/IVA on
increasing chloride transport is expected to be similar to TEZ/D-IVA (i.e., 18 µM/1 µM).

In Vitro Electrophysiology Data to Support Effectiveness of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA on Select
CFTR Variants
As mentioned above, 257 rare CFTR variants are proposed to be responsive to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
based on in vitro electrophysiology data. Except for 31 variants that were previously found not to
respond to ELX/TEZ/IVA, all are included in the ELX/TEZ/IVA label, either currently or
proposed in the efficacy supplement under review (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012
& 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]; source:
U032; ELX/TEZ/IVA-responsive variants are listed in p289v4 and U032). The Division of
Applied Regulatory Science (DARS) from the Office of Clinical Pharmacology thus focused on
these 31 CFTR variants for data analysis.
Study Design: Methods of Ussing Chamber Assay
Ussing chamber assays were performed to determine the baseline level of CFTR-mediated
chloride transport for mutants and wild-type CFTR proteins, as well as the effect of treatment in
increasing CFTR-mediated chloride transport. For CFTR variants included in U015, VNZ was
tested at 6 concentrations (0, 0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, and 10 μM; note that only 3 μM data were submitted
when the FDA requested raw data for independent analysis through “Information Request” – see
below). TEZ (10 μM) and D-IVA (1 μM) were tested at one concentration each. The 3 μM
concentration of VNZ was chosen to determine responsiveness. When administered, VNZ, TEZ,
and D-IVA were added 18–24 hours prior to electrophysiology recording. During the recording,
CFTR-mediated chloride transport was evoked by adding forskolin (10 μM), then verified by
eliminating the recorded current using a cocktail of CFTR inhibitors. The current mediated by
CFTR channels is calculated as the peak forskolin-evoked response minus the minimum stable
chloride current that remained after adding the CFTR inhibitor cocktail (see Figure 7). The same
experimental design was used to study CFTR variants included in P289v4 and U032. For the
FRT system, CFTR variants are defined as responsive to treatment if they exhibit a ≥10 percent
point increase in chloride transport expressed as percent change of normal over baseline.
For U020, Ussing chamber assays were performed on HBE cells derived from CF donors who
were either homozygous or heterozygous for N1303K with a MF variant on the other allele that
does not produce CFTR protein and is not expected to respond to medications that affect CFTR
processing or function. For S525, Ussing chamber assays were performed on HBE cells derived
from CF donors who were either homozygous or heterozygous for F508del where the variant on
the other allele is a MF and does not produce CFTR protein, thus is not expected to respond to
medications that affect CFTR processing or function. The N1303K-HBE cells were treated with
18 μM TEZ/1 μM IVA, 3.3 μM ELX/18 μM TEZ/1 μM IVA, or 0.22 μM VNZ/18 μM TEZ/1
μM D-IVA for 18 – 24 hours prior to electrophysiology recording. The F/F- and F/MF-HBE
cells were treated with 18 μM TEZ/1 μM IVA for 18 – 24 hours. For electrophysiology
recording, chloride transport was evoked with forskolin then eliminated with the CFTR inhibitor
cocktail as stated above for the FRT system.

An Information Request was sent to the Applicant to obtain the raw electrophysiology records
for the following 15 CFTR variants previously demonstrated to not respond to ELX/TEZ/IVA
and wild-type CFTR channels:
•

For U015 that used FRT cells: H119R, G91R, R1066C, Y569C, Q452P, A559T, I506T,

For U020 that used HBE cells from CF donors homozygous or heterozygous for N1303K
variant, and for S525 that used HBE cells from CF donors homozygous or heterozygous for
F508del-variant, DARS had requested the original source waveforms for these experiments and
completed data analysis to support the current efficacy supplements for ELX/TEZ/IVA (refer to
the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 & 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11,
2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]; source: U032). DARS data analysis had been complete, leading
to the conclusion that results from U020 and S525 could be used as they are.
The submitted Ussing chamber data by the Applicant were organized in the following manner for
every experiment: a column of datapoints for time, and a column of datapoints for chloride
transport. Plotting data from these two columns enabled DARS’ electrophysiologists to visualize
the signal recorded from the epithelial cell layer for the duration of the recording. Figure 7 shows
an exemplar recording from one epithelial cell layer of A559T CFTR variant.
Figure 7. Transmembrane Current Recorded From the Cells That Expressed A559T Variant

Source: DARS reviewer’s analysis
Forskolin was added elevate cAMP level, leading to activation of the CFTR channels. CFTR inhibitors were then added to eliminate
the current. CFTR current amplitude was estimated as the difference between the maxima and minima of the recorded current. Red
arrows indicate signals used to assess transmembrane resistance, a data quality metric to ensure preparation health.
Abbreviations: cAMP, cyclic adenosine monophosphate; CFTR, cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator;

These Ussing chamber experiments are high throughput in nature, with each execution testing 96
different epithelial cell layers on a recording plate that can be of different CFTR variants and/or
treated with different drug combinations. For each variant studied using the FRT system and
presented in U015, six experimental replicates were performed, each in a 96-well plate and with
three to four technical replicates to assess drug effect and two to 20 technical replicates to
measure baseline (no drug) response. The Applicant submitted raw data for recordings on the
recording plate, and a plate map for unblinding to identify which recordings corresponded to

CFTR variants identified in U015. DARS electrophysiologists were blinded to treatment

steps are as follows: 1) reconstruct the electrical waveforms and check data quality to verify
epithelial cell membrane integrity; 2) quantify treatment effects using DARS own criteria, and 3)
after extracting chloride transport measurements, unblind using the plate maps to summarize
drug effects on each variant. This process evaluates whether any electrophysiologist would arrive
at the same conclusion as the Applicant based on examining the original recorded signals alone.

Justification of Effect of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA on CFTR NCS Variants
Nineteen NCS variants are proposed to be responsive to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Among them, three
are supported by clinical data with ELX/TEZ/IVA. Sixteen ultra-rare ones, however, have
neither clinical data nor Ussing chamber HBE or FRT data. In general, splice variants result in
errors during the mRNA splicing process that then leads to improper intron retention or exon
removal. The reason for the lack of FRT data for these splice variants is that the FRT system
relies on CFTR cDNAs exogenously delivered by DNA vectors. The CFTR cDNA sequences do
not contain introns, and the system is not suitable to evaluate errors during the mRNA splicing
process. These 16 NCS variants are thought to produce a limited amount of wild-type CFTR
proteins based on some clinical phenotype results. In vitro results showed that wild-type CFTR
proteins have high baseline activity level and respond robustly to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment
(167% increase in chloride transport over normal in U015, in contrast to the 70%-point increase
in chloride transport over normal for ELX/TEZ/IVA in Report P289v4) may be used to support
that these splice variants would benefit from VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment. This approach is
consistent with the rationale to expand the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA to this group of variants
(refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 & 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on
December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]).

CFTR Variants That Were Evaluated in Clinical Trials for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
The Applicant stated in the “Clinical Overview-US” (p. 30, first paragraph) that for the 24 CFTR
variants evaluated in the phase 3 clinical program (F508del and 25 other ELX/TEZ/IVAresponsive variants), there are also data demonstrating response to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in the FRT
system. Variants studied in the phase 3 clinical program include the following:
•

A445E, G1244E, H1054D, L1077P, R1066H, R75Q, S549N, V562I, W1282R, D1152H,
G551D, I336K, L206W, S1159F, S549R, V754M, Y563N, F508del, G85E, M1101K, R352Q,
S1251N, S945L, and W1098C

Therefore, U015 study report does not contain effects of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA for these variants
studied in the clinical trials. An updated comparison to capture nonclinical-to-clinical data
translation is unlikely needed.
Western Blot Analysis
As stated above in “Regulatory history”, the Applicant also submitted Western Blot data to
demonstrate the effect of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA on the CFTR variants. Although useful to understand
mechanism-of-action for ELX, TEZ, and VNZ, Western Blot data have not been used to identify
treatment-responsive variants because an increase in mature CFTR proteins does not necessarily
translate to an increase in function.
DARS analyzed the Western Blot data submitted in U015 for the 31 variants responsive to
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA that were not responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA. The ratios of mature to total
CFTR proteins indicate that the treatment was effective at improving the maturation of CFTR for
most variants (Figure 10, left panel). The relationship between the changes induced
VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in maturation of CFTR proteins and increase in chloride transport was also
analyzed. The right panel of Figure 10 shows that the Western Blot and Ussing chamber data are
not strictly correlated, supporting the choice of Ussing chamber functional data over Western
Blot data to identify treatment-responsive variants.

Conclusions
(1) In vitro electrophysiology data obtained in the HBE and FRT systems support functional
equivalence of IVA and D-IVA on increasing chloride transport.
(2) Individual drug components of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA contribute to the overall effect of the new
triple combination on increasing chloride transport.
(3) The original electrophysiology source waveforms obtained from CFTR expressed in FRT
cell for 15 variants previously shown not to respond to ELX/TEZ/IVA have been received
and independently analyzed by DARS. These recordings are of good quality, performed on
uncompromised epithelial cell layers based on transmembrane resistance quality check, and
drug effects of VNZ/TEZ/IVA are robust and accurate as reported in U015 nonclinical study
report.
(4) Note that original electrophysiology source waveforms for the HBE experiments performed
on F508del- and N1303K-containing cells have already been analyzed to support the current
efficacy supplements for ELX/TEZ/IVA (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 &
217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]). DARS
had concluded that these data are adequate and accurate as reflected in the nonclinical study
reports U020 and S525.
(5) Wild-type CFTR channels respond to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The results of wild-type CFTR have
been received and independently analyzed by DARS. These recordings are of good quality,
performed on uncompromised epithelial cell layers based on transmembrane resistance
quality check, and drug effects of VNZ/TEZ/IVA are robust and accurate as reported in
U015 nonclinical study report.

(6) The rationale for extrapolating wild-type CFTR protein’s response toward the new triple
combination treatment for the 19 NCS variants is logical from DARS perspective.
(7) The increases in chloride transport elicited by VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA are generally numerically
greater than those elicited by ELX/TEZ/IVA. In vitro electrophysiology data support the
clinical conclusion that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA combination is noninferior in correcting CFTR
dysfunction in comparison with ELX/TEZ/IVA combination.

14.2.1. Single and Multiple Ascending Dose Studies
14.2.1.1. Study VX17-121-001 (Study 001)
Title
A Phase 1/2 Study of VX-121 [VNZ] in Healthy Subjects and in Subjects With Cystic Fibrosis
Study Period
March 20, 2018 to May 3, 2019
Objective
Primary Objectives
•

Part A: To evaluate the safety and tolerability of single ascending doses of VNZ in healthy
subjects

•

Part B: To evaluate the safety and tolerability of multiple ascending doses of VNZ for 10
days in healthy subjects

•

Part C: To evaluate the safety and tolerability of multiple ascending doses of VNZ
administered in TC with tezacaftor (TEZ)/ivacaftor (IVA) for 14 days in healthy subjects

•

Part D: To evaluate the safety and tolerability of VNZ/TEZ/IVA in subjects with CF

Study Design
A 4-part, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single- and multiple-dose, first-inhuman and first-in-patient dose escalation study of VNZ that included an evaluation of relative
bioavailability (BA) and the effect of milk on VNZ exposure.
Part A, B, and C
Parts A (exclusive of Cohort A9), B, and C were randomized, double-blind, and placebocontrolled study. Subjects were randomized 3:1 to receive either active study drugs or placebo.
Parts A (exclusive of Cohort A9) and B had a dose-escalation design, with each subject
participating in only one cohort (unless at least 21 days had elapsed before participation in
another cohort), to avoid PK and/or pharmacodynamic (PD) carryover effects (Figure 11).
Cohort A9: Subjects in Cohort A9 received three single doses of VNZ to evaluate the BA of a
tablet (with calcium restrictions) relative to the suspension formulation (with calcium
restrictions) and the effect of milk on the tablet formulation. Each dosing occasion was separated
by 8 days.
Part C evaluated the safety, tolerability, and PK of VNZ administered in TC with TEZ/IVA to
support potential development of this TC for the treatment of CF. The PK of TEZ and

145
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
metabolites, and IVA and metabolites was also evaluated, following administration of
VNZ/TEZ/IVA.
Figure 11. Study Design for Parts A, B, and C (Healthy Subjects), Study 001

Source: Figure 2-1 CSR VX17-121-001
a In addition to randomized study drug administration depicted above, after an 8-day washout, all subjects received an additional 5mg single dose of open-label VX-121 [VNZ] suspension with milk to evaluate the effect of milk on VX-121 [VNZ] exposure.
b Subjects received a single dose of VX-121 [VNZ] on three dosing occasions with an 8-day washout between dosing occasions.
VX-121 [VNZ] doses included a single oral dose of suspension, a single oral dose of tablet to evaluate the relative BA of the tablet,
and a single oral dose of tablet with milk to evaluate the effect of milk on VX-121 [VNZ] exposure.
c The dosage of TEZ/IVA was TEZ 100mg qd/IVA 150 mg q12h, which was administered as TEZ 100-mg/IVA 150-mg FDC in the
morning and IVA 150 mg in the evening.
Abbreviations: BA, bioavailability; FDC, fixed-dose combination; IVA, ivacaftor; q12h, every 12 hours; qd, once daily; TC, triple
combination; TEZ, tezacaftor; VX-121 [VNZ], vanzacaftor

Part D evaluated the safety, tolerability, PK, PD, and efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/IVA in CF subjects
who are heterozygous for F508del and a MF CFTR variants (F/MF genotype) (Figure 12).
Because there was no commercially available effective treatment for this population, a placebo
arm was included as the control treatment to assess whether any observed effects were treatmentrelated or simply related to the study conditions.

Conclusions
Parts A, B, and C (Healthy Subjects)
VNZ was generally safe and well tolerated at single doses up to 60 mg, at multiple doses up to
60 mg QD for 10 days, and at multiple doses up to 20 mg QD in TC with TEZ/IVA for 14 days.
When administered as single and multiple doses as a monotherapy, and in TC with TEZ/IVA,
VNZ exposure was approximately dose proportional. Based on observed Ctrough levels, VNZ
reached steady-state by Day 10. The mean accumulation ratio of AUCtau ranged from 3.5 to 5.5
following QD administration of VNZ. VNZ exposures in TC with TEZ/IVA were generally
consistent with those observed following VNZ monotherapy and also were consistent with
historical exposures.
Part D (CF Subjects)
VNZ/TEZ/IVA for 4 weeks was generally safe and well tolerated in CF subjects. VNZ, TEZ, and
IVA exposures in CF subjects were comparable to those in healthy subjects.
14.2.1.2. Study VX18-561-001 (Study 561-001)
Title
A Phase 1, Randomized, Double-blind, Placebo-controlled, Multiple-ascending Dose Study
Evaluating the Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of VX-561 [D-IVA] in Healthy
Subjects
Study Period
September 28, 2018 to November 16, 2018
Objective
•

To evaluate the PK of deutivacaftor (D-IVA) after administration of multiple ascending
doses of D-IVA in healthy subjects

•

To evaluate the safety and tolerability of multiple ascending doses of D-IVA in healthy
subjects
150

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5500284

NDA 218730
ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
Study Design
This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple-dose, dose escalation study of
D-IVA in healthy subjects. Subjects were randomized with 3 (active) to 1 (placebo) ratio. Fixedsequence study design was chosen to reduce the intersubject variability on PK endpoints.
Placebo was chosen as the control treatment to assess whether any observed effects were
treatment-related or simply related to the study condition. Study scheme is presented in Figure
13.
Figure 13. Study Design, Study 561-001

Source: Figure 9-1 CSR VX18-561-001
Abbreviations: D-IVA, deutivacaftor; qd, once daily

Study drug details are presented in Table 76. Study drug was administered orally in a fed state
that was 30 minutes after the start of breakfast containing approximately 20 g of fat.
Table 76. Study Drug, Study 561-001

Source: Table 9-2 CSR VX18-561-001
Abbreviations: D-IVA, deutivacaftor

Samples
Blood samples for plasma PK analysis were collected on Days 1, 5, 6, 10, 11, and 15 predose (0
hours) and at 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, 24 hours postdose. Additional 24 (Day 16), 36, 48 (Day 17),
and 72 hours (Day 18) post day 15 dose.
Results
A summary of D-IVA, D-M1, and D-M6 PK parameters following multiple doses of 50, 150,
and 250 mg QD of D-IVA are presented in Table 77. D-IVA exposure increased proportionally
with ascending doses from 50 to 250 mg QD. The D-IVA t1/2 was approximately 22 hours. DIVA tmax ranged from 3.0 to 6.0 hours. Steady-state appeared to be reached by Day 5.
Accumulation ratios for D-IVA AUCtau ranged from 1.22- to 1.73-fold across doses after 5 days
of dosing. Mean metabolite-to-parent ratios were similar across doses and were approximately
1.17 to 1.25 for M1-D-IVA at steady-state.

14.2.2. Relative Bioavailability Studies
14.2.2.1. Study VX16-770-018 (Study 018; Part D)
Title
A Phase 1, 4-part, Randomized, Open-label Study to Evaluate the Relative Bioavailability and
Food Effect of VX-561 [D-IVA] and Extended Release Ivacaftor Formulations in Healthy Adult
Subjects
Study Period
December 15, 2016 to June 20, 2017
Objective
Primary Objective
To evaluate the relative BA of D-IVA formulations compared to the immediate release (IR)
(commercial) formulation of IVA in the fed condition in healthy subjects
Secondary Objective
To assess the safety and tolerability of the D-IVA formulations in healthy subjects
Study Design
Part D was a 3-period, 3-sequence, crossover study to assess the relative BA of two D-IVA
tablets relative to the IVA IR (commercial) tablet.
A single oral dose of IVA IR tablet, D-IVA Formulation 1 (D-IVA F1; low drug load tablet), or
D-IVA Formulation 2 (D-IVA F2; high drug load tablet) was administered on the morning of the
first day of each dosing period (Days 1, 8, and 15). Dosing periods were separated by a 7-day
washout. The Safety Follow-up Visit assessments were performed on the last day of Dosing
Period 4 (Day 21). The study scheme is presented in Figure 14.

drug load tablet of D-IVA (F1).

14.2.2.2. Study VX19-121-003 (Study 003)
Title
A Phase 1, Open-label, Randomized, Crossover Study to Evaluate the Relative Bioavailability of
a Fixed-dose Combination Tablet of VX-121 [VNZ], Tezacaftor, and Deutivacaftor in Healthy
Adult Subjects
Study Period
August 4, 2020 to September 26, 2020
Objective
Primary Objective
To evaluate the relative BA of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA administered as a FDC tablet compared to
separate tablets of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA, under fed conditions.
Secondary Objective
To evaluate the safety and tolerability of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA administered as an FDC tablet or
separate tablets.
Study Design
This was a phase 1, open-label, randomized, 2-sequence, 2-period crossover study to evaluate the
relative BA of an FDC tablet of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (test formulation) compared to VNZ, TEZ,
and D-IVA coadministered as separate tablets (reference formulation). The FDC tablet (test
formulation) utilized the VNZ Form D physical formulation, while the separate tablet (reference
formulation) utilized the VNZ Form A physical formulation.
At the start of each Dosing Period, healthy adult subjects received a single dose of VNZ 20 mg,
TEZ 100 mg, and D-IVA 150 mg orally in the fed state, either as an FDC tablet or as separate
tablets (Figure 15).

The effect of food (high-fat and low-fat) on VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA PK after a single oral dose of

compared to fasted conditions was statistically evaluated. The resultant GLSM ratios and their
